a\boldsymbol{t}^{(n)}+(1-a)\boldsymbol{t}^{(n+1)}
will be “tangent” at the
n
-th elbow of the
\boldsymbol{p}
majorisation curve. The fact that
\boldsymbol{t}^{(n)}
may be a quasi-probability distribution does not pose a problem, since this vector can be projected back onto the probability simplex. The projected vector
\boldsymbol{t}^{(n)}
will be denoted by
\boldsymbol{t}^{(n)}_{\text{proj}}
, and can be obtained by successively applying the map

\left\{t^{(n)}_{m-1},\,t^{(n)}_{m}\right\}\longmapsto\left\{\min\left(t^{(n)}_{m-1}+t^{(n)}_{m},\,t^{(n)}_{m-1}\right),\,\max\left(t^{(n)}_{m},\,0\right)\right\}

(6)

to pairs of entries of
\boldsymbol{t}^{(n)}
going from
m=d
to
m=2
. In each step, the map either zeros the second component by shifting its value to the first one or, if the second component is non-negative, it leaves them both unperturbed. Geometrically, the state is shifted along the edges of the future cone of
\boldsymbol{t}^{(n)}
and every time it hits a plane defining one of the faces of the probability simplex
\Delta_{d}
, a new direction is selected, until the state is composed exclusively of non-negative entries.

Using Lemma
1.2
, we can now prove the following theorem that specifies the past cone.

{marginfigure}
[-0.493cm]

Majorisation cones.
Past region for a state with population
\boldsymbol{p}=(0.7,0.2,0.1)
.

Theorem 1.3

(Past cone)
.

The past cone of
\boldsymbol{p}
is given by

\mathcal{T}_{-}(\boldsymbol{p})=\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{d}\backslash\operatorname{int}(\mathbb{T})\,.

(7)

Proof.

One only needs to use the fact that

\mathcal{T}_{-}(\boldsymbol{p})=\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{d}\texttt{\textbackslash}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\emptyset}(\boldsymbol{p})\cup\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p})\right)\,,

(8)

and employ Lemma
1.2
to replace
\mathcal{T}_{\emptyset}(\boldsymbol{p})
in the above with Eq. (
5
).
∎

Let us make a few comments on the above results. First, note that the incomparable region arises only for
d\geq 3
. This can be easily deduced from Lemma
1.2
, as for
d=2
the two extreme points,
\boldsymbol{t}^{(1)}
and
\boldsymbol{t}^{(2)}
, are precisely the initial state
\boldsymbol{p}
. Second, the future thermal cone is symmetric with respect to the maximally mixed distribution
\boldsymbol{\eta}
, and consequently, the incomparable and past cones also exhibit a particular symmetry around this point. As we shall see, this symmetry is lost when we go beyond the limit of infinite temperature. Third, although the past cone is not convex as a whole, it is convex when restricted to any single Weyl chamber.
Therefore, we may note that the tangent vectors
\boldsymbol{t}^{(n)}
provide the extreme points of the past not only from the viewpoint of a single-chamber but also to the entire probability simplex. This can be understood by noting that
\boldsymbol{t}^{(n)}
are located at the boundary between the incomparable and the past cone, and by symmetry, it applies to all their permuted versions. As a consequence, the past is constructed from
d!
copies of the past in the canonical Weyl chamber, each copy transformed according to the corresponding permutation
\boldsymbol{\pi}
(see Fig.
1
a, b
).
Finally, one can make an analogy to special relativity with bistochastic matrices imposing a causal structure in the probability simplex
\Delta_{d}
. There exists a “light cone” for each point in
\Delta_{d}
, which divides the space into past, incomparable, and future regions (see Fig.
1
c
).

Figure 1:
Majorisation cones and Weyl chambers
. (a) Probability simplex
\Delta_{3}
and a state
\boldsymbol{p}=(0.6,0.3,0.1)
represented by a black dot
\bullet
together with its majorisation cones. The division of
\Delta_{3}
into different Weyl chambers is indicated by dashed lines with the central state
\boldsymbol{\eta}=(1/3,1/3,1/3)
denoted by a black star
\bigstar
. (b) The past cone of a state
\boldsymbol{p}
restricted to a given Weyl chamber is convex with the extreme points given by
\boldsymbol{t}^{(n)}
from Eq. (
2
) and the sharp state. (c) The causal structure induced by bistochastic matrices (i.e., thermal operations in the infinite temperature limit) in a given Weyl chamber.

{kaobox}

[frametitle= Constructing the incomparable region]
To gain geometric intuition for interpreting Lemma
1.2
, we consider a three-level system and construct its incomparable region.

Let
\boldsymbol{p}=(0.7,0.2,0.1)
be the population of the initial state. Then, Lemma (
1.2
) implies the existence of three different quasi-probability distributions
\boldsymbol{t}^{(n)}
, namely
\boldsymbol{t}^{(1)}=(0.7,0.7,-0.4)
,
\boldsymbol{t}^{(2)}=(0.7,0.2,0.1)
, and
\boldsymbol{t}^{(3)}=(0.8,0.1,0.1)
. The next step is to evaluate Eq. (
4
), i.e., calculate the future thermal cones for each quasi-probability distribution individually, and then take the convex union of all the cones. This process is illustrated below.

To obtain the incomparable region, we first take the set difference between the interior of
\mathbbm{T}
and the future thermal cone of
\boldsymbol{p}

The next step to compute Eq. (
5
) is to intersect with
\Delta_{d}
. Note that this procedure eliminates improper probability distributions. By doing so, we obtain the incomparable region, which is illustrated in Fig.
1.2
a.

The central idea behind Lemma
1.2
and Theorem
1.3
can be better understood through a visualisation using partial-order diagrams. To illustrate the principles of such diagrams, we will first focus on the special case of a three-level system. In this case, the past cone has three non-trivial extreme points:
\boldsymbol{t}^{(1)}
,
\boldsymbol{t}^{(2)}
and
\boldsymbol{t}^{(3)}
. Furthermore, as shown in Fig.
1
a
, these extreme points satisfy the following partial-order relation: the sharp state
\boldsymbol{s}_{1}
majorises both
\boldsymbol{t}^{(1)}
and
\boldsymbol{t}^{(3)}
, and these two vectors majorise the initial state
\boldsymbol{p}=\boldsymbol{t}^{(2)}
. As it was proved in Lemma
1.2
, the union of the future cones of these extreme points provides us, after subtracting the future of the vector
\boldsymbol{p}
, with the incomparable region of
\boldsymbol{p}
. In the particular case of
d=3
since
\boldsymbol{t}^{(1)},\,\boldsymbol{t}^{(3)}\succ\boldsymbol{t}^{(2)}
, we find that
\operatorname{conv}[\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(1)}),\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(2)})]=\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(1)})
and similarly for
\boldsymbol{t}^{(3)}
. However, it is important to note the fact that
\boldsymbol{t}^{(1)}
and
\boldsymbol{t}^{(3)}
are incomparable, and in turn, their respective future cones after subtracting future of
\boldsymbol{p}
characterise disjoint parts of the incomparable region. Finally, the tangent vector
\boldsymbol{t}^{(2)}
reduces to the original probability vector,
\boldsymbol{t}^{(2)}=\boldsymbol{p}
, only for
d=3
, and this fact is fully understood from the construction of the
\boldsymbol{t}^{(n)}
-vectors (see Section
5
).

{marginfigure}
[-5cm]

Hasse diagrams
. Graphical representation of the partially ordered set formed by the extreme points of the past cone in the probability simplex
\Delta_{d}
. Each arrow indicates that one of the elements precedes the other in the majorisation ordering. (a) Diagram for
d=3
. (b) Diagram for
d=4
. Note that any convex combination between
\boldsymbol{t}^{(2)}_{\text{proj}}
and
\boldsymbol{t}^{(3)}
, here denoted by
\boldsymbol{t}^{(2,3)}_{\lambda}
, results in an incomparable vector with respect to
\boldsymbol{t}^{(1)}_{\text{proj}}
and
\boldsymbol{t}^{(4)}
.

It is evident from Lemma
1.2
that each pair of tangent vectors
(\boldsymbol{t}^{(n)},\,\boldsymbol{t}^{(n+1)})
characterises a given part of the incomparable region. However, notice that the futures of the extreme points considered one by one do not give the full description of the incomparable region – one needs to consider their convex hulls to fill in the gaps. This particular feature of the construction can be demonstrated by considering the case of
d=4
. Observe that in this case, we have a set of four tangent vectors
\boldsymbol{t}^{(n)}
with
n\in\{1,2,3,4\}
. Straightforward calculation shows that
\boldsymbol{t}^{(1)}
majorises
\boldsymbol{t}^{(2)}
and
\boldsymbol{t}^{(4)}
majorises
\boldsymbol{t}^{(3)}
, therefore we find certain simplification, namely
\operatorname{conv}[\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(1)}),\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(2)})]=\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(1)})
and similarly for
\boldsymbol{t}^{(3)}
and
\boldsymbol{t}^{(4)}
. Nevertheless,
\boldsymbol{t}^{(1)}
is incomparable to
\boldsymbol{t}^{(4)}
; similarly
\boldsymbol{t}^{(2)}
belongs to the incomparable region of
\boldsymbol{t}^{(3)}
[see Fig.
1
b
].
From this we find the non-inclusions
\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(1)})\not\subset\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(4)})
and
\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(4)})\not\subset\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(1)})
, similarly
\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(2)})\not\subset\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(3)})
and
\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(3)})\not\subset\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(2)})
. Naively, one may be led to a conclusion that the incomparable region can be characterised by the future cones of
\boldsymbol{t}^{(1)}
and
\boldsymbol{t}^{(4)}
alone. However, any convex combination
\lambda\boldsymbol{t}^{(2)}+(1-\lambda)\boldsymbol{t}^{(3)}\equiv\boldsymbol{t}^{(2,3)}_{\lambda}
results in an incomparable vector
\boldsymbol{t}^{(2,3)}_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{T}_{\emptyset}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(i)})
for
i=1,2,3,4
and
0<\lambda<1

[see Fig.
1
c
], and hence, in a “new” fragment of the incomparable region. In order to account for the entire incomparable region one must take the union of all future cones of
\boldsymbol{t}^{(2,3)}_{\lambda}
for
\lambda\in[0,1]
. This corresponds to the convex hull of the future cones
1
1

1

Geometrically, the mixture of
\boldsymbol{t}^{(2)}
and
\boldsymbol{t}^{(3)}
corresponds to the edge that connects these two points.

\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(2)})
and
\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(3)})
. Furthermore, the construction is limited only to convex combinations of futures for consecutive tangent vectors since mixtures of two non-successive ones, for instance
\boldsymbol{t}^{(1)}
and
\boldsymbol{t}^{(4)}
, do not give any additions to the incomparable region. Such combinations belong to the past cone of
\boldsymbol{p}
as every point of
\lambda\boldsymbol{t}^{(1)}+(1-\lambda)\boldsymbol{t}^{(4)}
would majorise
\boldsymbol{p}
.

Subsection 1
Links to other resource theories

The two well-known examples of majorisation-based resource theories, where our results are also applicable, include the resource theories of entanglement and coherence. These are defined via the appropriate sets of free operations and free states: local operations and classical communication (LOCC) and separable states in entanglement theory
[
163
]
, and incoherent operations (IO) and incoherent states in coherence theory
[
159
]
. Within each of these theories, there exists a representation of quantum states via probability distributions that is relevant for formulating state interconversion conditions under free operations. In entanglement theory, a pure bipartite state
\rho=\outerproduct{\Psi}{\Psi}
can be written in terms of the Schmidt decomposition given by
\ket{\Psi}=\sum_{i}a_{i}\ket{\psi_i, \psi'_i}
, and represented by a probability vector
\boldsymbol{p}
with
p_{i}=|a_{i}|^{2}
. Then, Nielsen’s theorem
[
87
]
states that an initial state
\boldsymbol{p}
can be transformed under LOCC into a target state
\boldsymbol{q}
if and only if
\boldsymbol{p}\prec\boldsymbol{q}
. Similarly, in the resource theory of coherence with respect to a fixed basis
\{\ket{i}\}
, one can represent a pure state
\rho=\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}
by a probability vector
\boldsymbol{p}
with
p_{i}=|\langle i|\psi\rangle|^{2}
. Then, a given initial state
\boldsymbol{p}
can be transformed into
\boldsymbol{q}
via incoherent operations if and only if
\boldsymbol{p}\prec\boldsymbol{q}

[
91
]
.

{marginfigure}
[0cm]

Achiviebility under LOCC
. Entanglement cone in the simplex of the Schmidt coefficients of a
3\times 3
system. Conversely to thermodynamics, the past of entanglement transformations is the thermodynamic future and vice-versa. The black dot
\bullet
indicates the Schmidt vector of the initial state
\boldsymbol{p}=(0.7,0.2,0.1)
, whereas the black star
\bigstar
represents the maximally entangled state
\boldsymbol{\eta}=(1/3,1/3,1/3)

Therefore, we observe that the partial order emerging in the two cases is precisely the opposite to the thermodynamic order in the infinite temperature limit (for more details see Ref.
[
164
]
). Consequently, the thermodynamic past and future become the future and past for entanglement and coherence, while the incomparable region remains unchanged (see Fig.
1
). Note that, for entanglement and coherence, sharp states
\boldsymbol{s}
are in the future cone of any given state, while for thermodynamics (at
\beta=0
), they are in the past. The flat distribution
\boldsymbol{\eta}
is in the past of any state in entanglement and coherence theories, whereas in thermodynamics it is in the future.

One can make a general remark concerning resource monotones, applying to the entanglement, coherence and thermodynamic scenarios alike. Consider an entangled state
\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}_{N}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{N}
with the associated Schmidt coefficients
\boldsymbol{p}
and concurrence
\mathcal{C}(\ket{\psi})
as an example of a resource monotone
[
165
]
. If another state
\ket{\phi}
with Schmidt coefficients
\boldsymbol{q}
is in the future cone of
\ket{\psi}
,
\boldsymbol{q}\in\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p})
, then
\mathcal{C}(\ket{\phi})\leq\mathcal{C}(\ket{\psi})
. Otherwise, if it lies in its past cone,
\boldsymbol{q}\in\mathcal{T}_{-}(\boldsymbol{p})
we know that
\mathcal{C}(\ket{\phi})\geq\mathcal{C}(\ket{\psi})
. However, if the two states are incomparable,
\boldsymbol{q}\in\mathcal{T}_{\emptyset}(\boldsymbol{p})
, nothing can be said about the relation between both concurrences.

Subsection 2
Probabilistic majorisation cones

Finally, it should be observed that the notion of majorisation cones, as presented until now, deals with deterministic transformations. However, this approach can be extended to probabilistic transformations using Vidal’s criterion for entanglement
[
166
,
104
]
and coherence transformations
[
167
]
under LOCC and IO, respectively. In the case of probabilistic transformations of bipartite entangled states under LOCC, this is captured by the following theorem
[
166
]
.

Theorem 1.4
.

Consider two bipartite pure states
\ket{\psi}
and
\ket{\phi}
, whose Schmidt decompositions are described by probability vectors
\boldsymbol{p}
and
\boldsymbol{q}
, respectively. The maximal transformation probability from
\ket{\psi}
to
\ket{\phi}
under LOCC is given by

\mathcal{P}(\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{q})=\min_{1\leq k\leq d}\frac{\sum_{j=k}^{d}p^{\downarrow}_{j}}{\sum_{j=k}^{d}q^{\downarrow}_{j}}.

(9)

Figure 2:
Probabilistic majorisation cones for
d=3
. For a three-level system with a state given by
\boldsymbol{p}=(0.7,0.2,0.1)
represented by a black dot
\bullet
and a maximally entangled state
\boldsymbol{\eta}=(1/3,1/3,1/3)
represented by a black star
\bigstar
, we plot its probabilistic majorisation cone for probabilities of transformation
\mathcal{P}
decreasing from
1
to
0.5
with
0.125
steps (a-e), respectively. Observe that, for
\mathcal{P}=1
, we recover the structure of the standard majorisation cones, while as
\mathcal{P}
decreases the interconvertible region
\mathcal{T}_{\leftrightarrow}(\boldsymbol{p},\mathcal{P})
expands and the incomparable region
\mathcal{T}_{\emptyset}(\boldsymbol{p},\mathcal{P})
shrinks, disappearing altogether between panel (d) and (e)

.

In Section
3
, we discuss the extension of majorisation cones to probabilistic ones, denoted as
\mathcal{T}_{i}(\boldsymbol{p};\mathcal{P})
, with
i\in\{-,\emptyset,+\}
and
\mathcal{P}
being the minimal probability of transformation. Here we will limit ourselves to a brief qualitative discussion
about the behaviour of the probabilistic majorisation cones as the transformation probability changes from
\mathcal{P}(\cdot,\cdot)=1
to
\mathcal{P}(\cdot,\cdot)<1
(see Fig.
2
). Note that the only common points of the future and past for
\mathcal{P}=1
are the current state of the system
\boldsymbol{p}
and its permutations. Conversely, for
\mathcal{P}<1
this is not the only case; consequently, we may define the
interconvertible region
of
\boldsymbol{p}
at the probability level
\mathcal{P}
as the intersection between the probabilistic past and probabilistic future,
\mathcal{T}_{\leftrightarrow}(\boldsymbol{p},\mathcal{P})\equiv\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p},\mathcal{P})\cap\mathcal{T}_{-}(\boldsymbol{p},\mathcal{P})
. This region is non-empty for every
\mathcal{P}<1
. It is easily shown that the future and the past cones grow as the probability of transformation decreases,
\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})\subset\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p},\mathcal{P})
and
\mathcal{T}_{-}(\boldsymbol{p},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})\subset\mathcal{T}_{-}(\boldsymbol{p},\mathcal{P})
for
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}>\mathcal{P}
. Therefore, the only region that decreases together with
\mathcal{P}
is the incomparable region,
\mathcal{T}_{\emptyset}(\boldsymbol{p},\mathcal{P}^{\prime})\supset\mathcal{T}_{\emptyset}(\boldsymbol{p},\mathcal{P})
. Interestingly, for every state
\boldsymbol{p}
, we observe that there is a critical value
\mathcal{P}^{*}
, at which no two states are incomparable, i.e.,
\mathcal{T}_{\emptyset}(\boldsymbol{p},\mathcal{P})=\emptyset
[see Figs.
2
a, e
]. Analogous results hold in the context of coherence, as Theorem
1.4
has its counterpart when considering pure state transformations under IO operations
[
167
,
168
]
.

Finally, it is worth mentioning, that a criterion similar to the Vidal’s criterion was established for probabilistic transformation in the context of thermal operations
[
169
]
. In this case, the construction of the probabilistic cones for majorisation generalises directly to the thermomajorisation by using the construction of thermomajorisation cones which we will introduce in the next section.

Section 2
Thermal cones

Let us turn our attention to a more general scenario, assuming that the temperature is finite,
\beta>0
. In this case, the reachability of energy-incoherent states under Gibbs-preserving matrices can be studied by introducing the notion of
thermal cones
, defined as follows:

Definition 2.1

(Thermal cones)
.

The set of states that an energy-incoherent state
\boldsymbol{p}
can be mapped into by Gibbs-preserving matrices is called the
future thermal cone

\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p})
. Similarly, the set of states that can be mapped to
\boldsymbol{p}
by Gibbs-preserving matrices is called the
past thermal cone

\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{-}(\boldsymbol{p})
. Finally, the set of states that are neither in the past nor in the future of
\boldsymbol{p}
is called the
incomparable thermal region

\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{\emptyset}(\boldsymbol{p})
.

Despite apparent similarities, the case of
\beta>0
turns out to be significantly harder than
\beta=0
. Difficulties stem mostly from a simple fact demonstrated in Ref.
[
153
]
– even though thermomajorisation forms a lattice in each
\beta
-order, it does not provide a common lattice for the entire probability simplex. Thus, before extending Lemma
1.2
and Theorem
1.3
to the thermal setting (proofs of which rely heavily on the existence of a join), we will introduce an
embedding lattice
– a structure which encompasses thermomajorisation order as its subset – and we will demonstrate operations shifting to and from the newly introduced picture.

Subsection 1
Embedding lattice

To define a
\beta
-dependent embedding
\mathfrak{M}
of the simplex
\Delta_{d}
into a subspace
\Delta^{\mathfrak{M}}_{d}\subset\Delta_{2^{d}-1}
, illustrated by a graphical example in Fig.
3
, we first introduce the vector
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\mathfrak{M}}
with entries given by all possible partial sums of the Gibbs distribution,

\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\mathfrak{M}}=\left\{\sum_{i\in I}\gamma_{i}:\,I\in 2^{\{1,\ldots,d\}}\right\},

(10)

with
2^{\{1,\ldots,d\}}
denoting the power set of
d
indices. Moreover, we enforce that it is ordered non-decreasingly, i.e., for
i>j
we have
\Gamma^{\mathfrak{M}}_{i}\geq\Gamma^{\mathfrak{M}}_{j}
. Then, the embedded probability vector,
\boldsymbol{p}^{\mathfrak{M}}:=\mathfrak{M}(\boldsymbol{p})
, is defined by

\displaystyle p^{\mathfrak{M}}_{i}

\displaystyle=f^{\,\beta}_{\boldsymbol{p}}\left(\Gamma^{\mathfrak{M}}_{i}\right)-f^{\,\beta}_{\boldsymbol{p}}\left(\Gamma^{\mathfrak{M}}_{i-1}\right),

(11)

where
f^{\,\beta}_{\boldsymbol{p}}
is the thermomajorisation curve of
\boldsymbol{p}
.

Within this embedding, the thermomajorisation indeed proves to be almost-standard majorisation relation between the embedded distributions,

\boldsymbol{p}\succ_{\beta}\boldsymbol{q}\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ \forall j:\penalty\ \sum_{i=1}^{j}p^{\mathfrak{M}}_{i}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{j}q^{\mathfrak{M}}_{i}\penalty\ \overset{\text{def}}{\Longleftrightarrow}\penalty\ \boldsymbol{p}^{\mathfrak{M}}\succ_{\mathfrak{M}}\boldsymbol{q}^{\mathfrak{M}},

(12)

where the last symbol
\succ_{\mathfrak{M}}
denotes the majorisation variant related to the embedding lattice. Finally, we note that the only deviation from the standard majorisation lies in the convexity condition in the embedding space, which is imposed not on the probabilities
p^{\mathfrak{M}}_{i}
themselves, but on their rescaled versions,

i\leq j\Rightarrow\frac{p^{\mathfrak{M}}_{i}}{\gamma^{\mathfrak{M}}_{i}}\geq\frac{p^{\mathfrak{M}}_{j}}{\gamma^{\mathfrak{M}}_{j}},

(13)

with scaling factors directly related to the embedded majorisation curve of the Gibbs state
\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{M}}
. This ordering should be compared (but not confused) with the
\beta
-ordering introduced in Eq. (
28
), pointing to a relation with thermomajorisation which we will use to show the lattice structure of the introduced space.

Figure 3:

Embedded majorisation curve
. Embedding
\mathfrak{M}:\Delta_{d}\rightarrow\Delta_{2^{d}-1}
of
d
-dimensional probability distribution
\boldsymbol{p}
(here
d=3
) into a
2^{d}-1=7
-dimensional space is most easily understood by noting that each thermomajorisation curve (left) has elbows corresponding to a subset of
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\mathfrak{M}}
on the horizontal axis. The embedding includes all entries of
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{\mathfrak{M}}
by subdividing the Lorenz curve into
2^{d}-1
fragments. Conversely, the projection
\mathfrak{P}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\boldsymbol{p}}}
corresponds to selecting only a subset of elbows that correspond to a selected order, in this case the original order
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\boldsymbol{p}}
. In the
x
-axes we used the shorthand notation
\gamma_{ij}=\gamma_{i}+\gamma_{j}
.

The projection
\mathfrak{P}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
of an arbitrary probability vector
\boldsymbol{q}\in\Delta_{2^{d}-1}
satisfying Eq. (
13
) onto a selected
\beta
-order
\boldsymbol{\pi}
in the original space can be defined descriptively as taking only those elbows of the embedded majorisation curve that match the values of cumulative Gibbs distribution for the selected permutation. Formally, the projected vector,
\boldsymbol{q}^{\mathfrak{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}:=\left(\mathfrak{P}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}\left(\boldsymbol{q}\right)\right)^{\,\beta}
, is entry-wise defined by

\left(q^{\mathfrak{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}\right)_{i}=\sum^{k(i)}_{j=k(i-1)}q_{j},

(14)

with the indices
k(i)
defined by the requirement that
\Gamma^{\mathfrak{M}}_{k(i)}=\sum_{j=1}^{i}\gamma_{\boldsymbol{\pi}^{-1}(j)}
.

In particular, it is worth noting two properties of the embedding
\mathfrak{M}
and projections
\mathfrak{P}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
. First, given a vector
\boldsymbol{p}\in\Delta_{d}
with a
\beta
-order
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\boldsymbol{p}}
, we find that by construction
\mathfrak{P}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\boldsymbol{p}}}\left(\mathfrak{M}\left(\boldsymbol{p}\right)\right)=\boldsymbol{p}
, which follows directly from Eq. (
14
). On the other hand, for
\boldsymbol{\pi}\neq\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\boldsymbol{p}}
we find that
\boldsymbol{p}\succ_{\beta}\mathfrak{P}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}\left(\mathfrak{M}\left(\boldsymbol{p}\right)\right)
. The statement is easily shown by observing that the Lorenz curve of
\mathfrak{P}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}\left(\mathfrak{M}\left(\boldsymbol{p}\right)\right)
connects by line segments
d+1
points of the Lorenz curve corresponding
f^{\beta}_{\boldsymbol{p}}\left(\sum_{i}\left(\mathfrak{M}(p)^{\mathfrak{P}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}\right)_{i}\right)
and therefore majorisation is resolved by linear approximation of a convex function,
f[(1-t)x+ty]\geq(1-t)f(x)+tf(y)
for any
x,y
. The second property is concerned with
\boldsymbol{q}\in\Delta_{2^{d}-1}
satisfying Eq. (
13
) and can be summarised as the fact that projecting and re-embedding the vector will always give the object majorised by the original vector:
\boldsymbol{q}\succ_{\mathfrak{M}}\mathfrak{M}(\mathfrak{P}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\boldsymbol{q}))
. It follows similarly to the prior majorisation by the argument of linear approximation of a convex function.

It is necessary to stress that the introduced embedding structure is distinct from the one used in the usual method of reducing thermomajorisation to majorisation
[
56
]
. Most importantly, the standard approach requires going to the limit of infinite embedding dimension in order to recover thermomajorisation for arbitrary
\beta
as a special case of standard majorisation. In our proposition, a thermomajorisation curve in dimension
d
is embedded within a
2^{d}-1
-dimensional space. The main difference lies in the non-constant widths of the segments of the Lorenz curve and the fact that once the embedded vector
\boldsymbol{p}^{\mathfrak{M}}
is constructed, its entries should not be subject to reordering. Finally, we present the argument proving that the embedding together with embedded majorisation indeed provide a lattice structure.

Corollary 2.1
.

The subset of the probability simplex
\Delta_{2^{d}-1}
satisfying Eq. (
13
) and subject to the embedded majorisation
\succ_{\mathfrak{M}}
defined in Eq. (
12
) forms a lattice.

Proof.

The embedded majorisation
\succ_{\mathfrak{M}}
may be reinterpretted as thermomajorisation defined for a specific Gibbs state
\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\mathfrak{M}}
and restricted to a particular Weyl chamber of the probability space
\Delta_{2^{d}-1}
by comparing Eq. (
13
) with an analogous sorting rule from Eq. (
28
). This direct isomorphism between thermomajorisation order
\succ_{\beta}
restricted to a single Weyl chamber, known to provide a lattice structure, and the embedded majorisation
\succ_{\mathfrak{M}}
proves the statement.
∎

Subsection 2
Geometry of thermal cones

As already mentioned, for finite temperatures the rules underlying state transformations are no longer captured by a majorisation relation, but rather by its thermodynamic equivalent known as thermomajorisation
[
170
,
56
]
. As a result, Birkhoff’s theorem cannot be employed anymore, and the characterisation of the future thermal cone is no longer given by Theorem
1.1
. However, the set of Gibbs-preserving matrices still forms a convex set
[
155
,
156
]
, and the extreme points of the future thermal cone can be constructed by employing the following lemma:

Lemma 2.2

(Lemma 12 of Ref.
[
154
]
)
.

Given
\boldsymbol{p}
, consider the following distributions
\boldsymbol{p}^{\,\boldsymbol{\pi}}\in\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p})
constructed for each permutation
\boldsymbol{\pi}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}
. For
i\in\left\{1,\dots,d\right\}
:

1.

Let
x_{i}^{\boldsymbol{\pi}}=\sum_{j=0}^{i}e^{-\beta E_{\boldsymbol{\pi}^{-1}\left(j\right)}}
and
y_{i}^{\boldsymbol{\pi}}=f^{\,\beta}_{\boldsymbol{p}}\left(x_{i}^{\boldsymbol{\pi}}\right)
.

2.

Define
p^{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{i}:=y^{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}(i)}-y^{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}(i)-1}
, with
y_{0}:=0
.

Then, all extreme points of
\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p})
have the form
\boldsymbol{p}^{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
for some
{\boldsymbol{\pi}}
. In particular, this implies that
\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p})
has at most
d!
extremal points.

The above lemma allows one to characterise the future thermal cone of
\boldsymbol{p}
by constructing states
\boldsymbol{p}^{\,\boldsymbol{\pi}}
for each
\boldsymbol{\pi}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}
, and taking their convex hull. It is worth mentioning that
\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p})
can also be constructed by finding the whole set of extremal Gibbs-preserving matrices
[
154
,
155
]
. This follows the same spirit as in Sec.
1
, where the majorisation cone was characterised by employing Theorem
3.1
. However, this is a harder problem to solve, and so the extremal Gibbs-preserving matrices were characterised only for
d\leq 3

[
155
,
156
]
. Here, we provide the construction of
\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p})
as a simple corollary of Lemma
2.2
.

Figure 4:
Thermal cones for
d=3
. For a three-level system with population given by
\boldsymbol{p}=(0.4,0.36,0.24)
, represented by a black dot
\bullet
, and energy spectrum
E_{1}=0,E_{2}=1
and
E_{3}=2
, we plot its thermal cone for (a)
\beta=0
, (b)
\beta=0.5
, (c)
\beta=1.0
and (d)
\beta\to\infty
. By increasing
\beta
, the thermal state (black star
\bigstar
) tends toward the ground state
E_{1}
, and the past thermal cone becomes convex.

Corollary 2.3

(Future thermal cone)
.

The future thermal cone of a
d
-dimensional energy-incoherent state
\boldsymbol{p}
is given by

\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p})=\operatorname{conv}[\{\boldsymbol{p}^{\boldsymbol{\pi}},\boldsymbol{\pi}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}\}]\,.

(15)

Furthermore, one can use the embedding lattice to provide an alternative formulation for the future thermal cone:

Observation 2.4
.

Since
\boldsymbol{p}^{\boldsymbol{\pi}}=\mathfrak{P}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\mathfrak{M}(\boldsymbol{p}))
, the future thermal cone of an energy-incoherent state
\boldsymbol{p}
can be expressed in terms of all possible projections from the related embedding,

\mathcal{T}^{\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p})=\operatorname{conv}\left[\left\{\mathfrak{P}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}}(\mathfrak{M}(\boldsymbol{p}))\,,\boldsymbol{\pi}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}\right\}\right].

(16)

Our main technical contribution is captured by the following Lemma that generalises Lemma
1.2
and provides the construction of the incomparable thermal region for finite temperatures. Its proof is based on the concept of embedding lattice that we introduced in Sec.
1
and can be found in Appendix
2

Lemma 2.5

(Incomparable thermal region)
.

Given an energy-incoherent state
\boldsymbol{p}
and a thermal state
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
, consider distributions
\boldsymbol{t}^{(n,\boldsymbol{\pi})}
in their
\beta
-ordered form, constructed for each permutation
\boldsymbol{\pi}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}
,

\left(\boldsymbol{t}^{(n,\boldsymbol{\pi})}\right)^{\,\beta}=\left(t^{(n,\boldsymbol{\pi})}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}(1)},p^{\,\beta}_{n}\frac{\gamma_{\boldsymbol{\pi}(2)}}{\gamma^{\,\beta}_{n}},...,p^{\,\beta}_{n}\frac{\gamma_{\boldsymbol{\pi}(d-1)}}{\gamma^{\,\beta}_{n}},t^{(n,\boldsymbol{\pi})}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}(d)}\right),

(17)

with

\displaystyle t^{(n,\boldsymbol{\pi})}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}(1)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}p^{\,\beta}_{i}-\frac{p^{\,\beta}_{n}}{\gamma^{\,\beta}_{n}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\gamma^{\,\beta}_{i}-\gamma_{\boldsymbol{\pi}(1)}\right),

(18a)

\displaystyle t^{(n,\boldsymbol{\pi})}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}(d)}

\displaystyle=1-t^{(n,\boldsymbol{\pi})}_{\boldsymbol{\pi}(1)}-\frac{p^{\,\beta}_{n}}{\gamma^{\,\beta}_{n}}\sum_{i=2}^{d-1}\gamma_{\boldsymbol{\pi}(i)}.

(18b)

Defining the set

\mathbb{T}^{\,\beta}=\bigcup_{\boldsymbol{\pi}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}}\bigcup_{i=1}^{n-1}\operatorname{conv}\left[\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{+}\left(\boldsymbol{t}^{(i,\boldsymbol{\pi})}\right)\cup\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{+}\left(\boldsymbol{t}^{(i+1,\boldsymbol{\pi})}\right)\right],

(19)

the incomparable region of
\boldsymbol{p}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{\emptyset}(\boldsymbol{p})=\left[\operatorname{int}\left(\mathbb{T}^{\,\beta}\right)\backslash\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p})\right]\cap\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{d}.

(20)

Figure 5:

Extreme points of
\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{-}(\boldsymbol{p})
. The past thermal cone for a three-level system with population give by
\boldsymbol{p}=(0.7,0.2,0.1)
, energy spectrum
E_{1}=0,E_{2}=2
and
E_{3}=3
, and the inverse temperature
\beta=0.5
. At each chamber
\boldsymbol{\pi}
, the non-trivial extreme points of the past are given by
\boldsymbol{t}^{(n,\boldsymbol{\pi})}
(for
n\in\{1,2,3\}
and
\boldsymbol{\pi}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}
) and by extreme points of
\bigcup_{i=1}^{d-1}\operatorname{conv}[(\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(i,\boldsymbol{\pi})})\cup\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(i+1,\boldsymbol{\pi})})]
(red dots). The remaining ones are sharp states, points in the boundary between chambers (green dots) and those which are on the edge of the probability simplex, i.e.,
(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i,j})_{k}=(\gamma_{i}\delta_{ik}+\gamma_{j}\delta_{ik})/(\gamma_{i}+\gamma_{j})
, for
i\neq j
, and
k\in\{1,2,3\}
.

Analogously to the infinite temperature case, Lemma
2.5
allows us to obtain the past thermal cone of
\boldsymbol{p}
.

Theorem 2.6

(Past thermal cone)
.

The past thermal cone of
\boldsymbol{p}
is given by

\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{-}(\boldsymbol{p})=\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{d}\backslash\operatorname{int}\left(\mathbb{T}^{\,\beta}\right)\,.

(21)

Proof.

Following the same reasoning as in the proof of Theorem
1.3
, we only need to use the fact that
\operatorname{int}(\mathbb{T}^{\beta})=\mathcal{T}^{\beta}_{+}(p)\cup\mathcal{T}^{\beta}_{\emptyset}(p)
.
∎

In Fig.
4
, we illustrate Lemma
2.5
and Theorem
2.6
for a three-level system. We also provide a
Mathematica
code
[
171
]
that constructs the set of extreme points of the future and past thermal cones for arbitrary dimensions.

As before, the past thermal cone forms a convex polytope only when restricted to a single Weyl chamber, now defined as a set of probability vectors with common
\beta
-order. The extreme points of the past thermal cone correspond to tangent vectors
\boldsymbol{t}^{(n,\boldsymbol{\pi})}
or by their projection onto the boundary of the probability simplex. The exceptional points in comparison with the infinite-temperature case may appear when considering extreme points of
\bigcup_{i=1}^{d-1}\operatorname{conv}[(\mathcal{T}^{\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(i,\boldsymbol{\pi})})\cup\mathcal{T}^{\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{t}^{(i+1,\boldsymbol{\pi})})]
for a given chamber
\boldsymbol{\pi}
. Vertices arising in this way may not correspond to any tangent vector (see Fig.
5
). Moreover, the
\boldsymbol{t}^{(n,\boldsymbol{\pi})}
vectors are also responsible for the convexity of the past thermal cone, and the following observation illustrates this:

Corollary 2.7

(Asymptotic temperature limit)
.

Approaching the limit of
\beta\to\infty
, the past thermal cone becomes convex.

Proof.

By dividing the probability simplex into equal chambers with the thermal state in the barycenter, the past thermal cone is the union of
d!
convex pieces. As
\beta\to\infty
, the thermal state collapses to the ground state. There is only one chamber in this limit, and therefore, the past thermal cone is a single convex piece [see Fig.
4
e
for an example in the particular case of
d=3
].
∎

The intuition behind the above observation can be understood by studying the behaviour of a three-level system and the tangent vector
\boldsymbol{t}^{(3,(132))}
. By a decreasing temperature, this extremal point tends towards the edge of the simplex and reaches the edge at
\beta\to\infty
[see Figs.
4
b, e
].

To wrap up the considerations of the geometry of thermal cones, let us go back, once again, to the analogy between the thermal cones and special relativity. Consider that given a specific division of space-time into future, past and space-like regions, one is able to recover the specific event generating it, which we may refer to as present. Concisely – there is a one-to-one relation between events and the divisions of space-time they generate. The situation is exactly reflected for thermal cones with
\beta>0
– given a specific arrangement of incomparable region and future and past thermal cones, one can exactly recover the current state of the system [see the black dot
\bullet
in Fig.
4
b-d
]. It is in stark contrast with the majorisation cones for
\beta=0
, where every division into past, future and incomparable possesses
d!
-fold symmetry [see Fig.
4
a
] and hence, the present state of the system cannot be recovered solely on its basis unless provided with additional information like the permutation which sorts the probabilities in non-decreasing order.

Section 3
Volume of thermal cones

In the previous sections, we characterised and discussed the behaviour of thermal cones by introducing explicit constructions of the past, the future and the incomparable region. It is then natural to ask what is the role played by their volumes in quantifying the resourcefulness of different states
[
172
,
168
]
. Thus, for a
d
-dimensional probability vector
\boldsymbol{p}
we define the relative volumes of its thermal cones as

\mathcal{V}^{\,\beta}_{i}(\boldsymbol{p}):=\frac{V\left[\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{i}(\boldsymbol{p})\right]}{V(\Delta_{d})}\quad\text{with}\quad i\in\{\emptyset,-,+\},

(22)

where
V
denotes the volume measured using the Euclidean metric.

We start our analysis of volumes of thermal cones by presenting an operational interpretation in terms of guessing probabilities for the future and past of a given state subject to a thermal evolution; such interpretation provides a solid basis for presenting the aforementioned volumes as resource-theoretic monotones. Subsequently, we proceed to an in-depth analysis of the volumes with a particular focus on their behaviour as a function of the inverse temperature
\beta
. Finally, we explain how to modify the analysis to obtain meaningful volumes of entanglement cones.

Subsection 1
Interpretation

Consider a task of predicting the future, which roughly translates to guessing a state
\boldsymbol{q}
by having knowledge that it has originated from a given prior state
\boldsymbol{p}
. In this case, the probability of correctly guessing a state that is
\epsilon
-distant from
{\boldsymbol{q}}
is given by

\text{Pr}\left({\boldsymbol{q}},\epsilon\,|\,\boldsymbol{q}\in\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p})\right)=\frac{V(\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{q}))}{\mathcal{V}^{\,\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p})},

(23)

where
\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{q})
is an
\epsilon
-ball centred at
\boldsymbol{q}
. We may get rid of the dependence on
\epsilon
and
\boldsymbol{q}
by taking the ratio for two different states
\boldsymbol{p}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{p}_{2}
,

\frac{\text{Pr}\left[\boldsymbol{q},\epsilon\,|\,\boldsymbol{q}\in\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p}_{2})\right]}{\text{Pr}\left[\boldsymbol{q},\epsilon\,|\,\boldsymbol{q}\in\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p}_{1})\right]}=\frac{\mathcal{V}^{\,\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p}_{1})}{\mathcal{V}^{\,\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p}_{2})}.

(24)

Thus, the ratio of volumes yields a relative probability of guessing the future of two different states. In particular, if
\boldsymbol{p}_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p}_{1})
, then also
\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p}_{2})\subset\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p}_{1})
and in consequence

\frac{\text{Pr}\left[\boldsymbol{q},\epsilon\,|\,\boldsymbol{q}\in\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p}_{2})\right]}{\text{Pr}\left[\boldsymbol{q},\epsilon\,|\,\boldsymbol{q}\in\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p}_{1})\right]}>1.

(25)

This can be understood as follows – as the evolution of a system progresses, the future becomes easier to guess or, in other words, more predictable.

In complete analogy, we may define a game in which, instead of guessing – or predicting – the future of a state
\boldsymbol{p}
, one has to guess its past. For such a game, it is easy to show that

\frac{\text{Pr}\left[\boldsymbol{q},\epsilon\,|\,\boldsymbol{q}\in\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{-}(\boldsymbol{p}_{2})\right]}{\text{Pr}\left[\boldsymbol{q},\epsilon\,|\,\boldsymbol{q}\in\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{-}(\boldsymbol{p}_{1})\right]}=\frac{\mathcal{V}^{\,\beta}_{-}(\boldsymbol{p}_{1})}{\mathcal{V}^{\,\beta}_{-}(\boldsymbol{p}_{2})},

(26)

so that the ratio of volumes yields the relative probability of guessing the past. Given
\boldsymbol{p}_{1}\in\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{-}(\boldsymbol{p}_{2})
we have
{\mathcal{V}^{\,\beta}_{-}(\boldsymbol{p}_{1})}/{\mathcal{V}^{\,\beta}_{-}(\boldsymbol{p}_{2})}<1
, which simply means that as the evolution towards equilibrium progresses, one finds the past of a given state harder and harder to guess correctly.

Subsection 2
Properties

We now show that the volumes of the thermal cones are thermodynamic monotones, i.e., functions of a state that decrease under thermal operations.

Theorem 3.1

(Thermodynamic monotones)
.

The relative volumes
\mathcal{V}^{\,\beta}_{+}
and
1-\mathcal{V}^{\,\beta}_{-}=\mathcal{V}^{\,\beta}_{+}+\mathcal{V}^{\,\beta}_{\emptyset}
are thermodynamic monotones. Moreover, both monotones are faithful, taking the value 0 only when applied to the Gibbs state
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
.

Proof.

One can straightforwardly show that both quantities decrease monotonically under thermodynamic operations. This is a simple consequence of the fact that for
\boldsymbol{p}
and
\boldsymbol{q}
connected via a thermal operation, we have
\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{q})\subset\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p})
and
\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{-}(\boldsymbol{p})\subset\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{-}(\boldsymbol{q})
which automatically implies
\mathcal{V}^{\,\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{q})\leq\mathcal{V}^{\,\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p})
and
\mathcal{V}^{\,\beta}_{-}(\boldsymbol{q})\geq\mathcal{V}^{\,\beta}_{-}(\boldsymbol{p})
.

In order to demonstrate its faithfulness, first note that every state that is not thermal can be mapped to a thermal state, thus showing that
1-\mathcal{V}_{-}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})=0
. Similarly, the Gibbs state cannot be mapped via thermal operations to anything else than itself, thus
\mathcal{V}_{+}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})=0
. Now, in order to show that both monotones are non-zero for any state different from the Gibbs state, it suffices to demonstrate that
\mathcal{V}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p})>0
for any
\boldsymbol{p}\neq\boldsymbol{\gamma}
. It is enough to consider, without loss of generality, a state
\boldsymbol{p}
thermalised within a
(d-1)
-dimensional subspace, i.e.,
{p_{i}}/{p_{j}}={\gamma_{i}}/{\gamma_{j}}
for all
i\neq j\neq 1
. For
k\in\{2,\ldots,d\}
, using
\beta
-swaps between levels
1
and
k
defined as
[
154
]

\left\{p_{1},\,p_{k}\right\}\longmapsto\left\{\frac{\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{k}}{\gamma_{1}}p_{1}+p_{k},\,\frac{\gamma_{k}}{\gamma_{1}}p_{1}\right\},

we generate
d-1
new points shifted from the original state by displacements
(\boldsymbol{\delta}_{k})_{i}=\delta_{k}\epsilon_{ik}
defined by the Levi-Civita symbol
\epsilon_{ik}
and
\delta_{k}\neq 0
. The entire set
\{\boldsymbol{\delta}_{k}\}_{k=2}^{d}
of displacements is linearly independent; therefore, they define a
(d-1)
-dimensional simplex of non-zero volume. Conversely, if we assume that
\delta_{k}=0
for any
k
, we are led to a conclusion that the system is thermalised between levels
1
and
k
and, by transitivity, it must be equal to the Gibbs state
\gamma
, which leads to a contradiction with the initial assumption. Finally, we restore the full generality by noticing that any state
\boldsymbol{q}
contains in its future cone states thermalised in any of the
(d-1)
-dimensional subspaces and, consequently, their entire future cone. Therefore, the non-zero volume of the latter implies the non-zero volume of the former.
∎

The behaviour of
\mathcal{V}_{+}
and
\mathcal{V}_{-}
as a function of
\beta
strongly depends on the
\beta
-ordering of the state under consideration. Among all
\beta
-orderings, there are two extreme cases, namely the one where the population and energies are arranged in non-increasing order (
p_{i}\leq p_{j}
for
E_{i}\leq E_{j}
), and the other in which the populations are ‘anti-ordered’ with respect to the energies (
E_{i}\leq E_{j}
implies
p_{i}\geq p_{j}
). These two distinct
\beta
-orderings characterise
passive
and
maximally active
states, respectively
[
173
,
174
]
. At the bottom left of Fig.
2
, for a three-level system, we depict each Weyl chamber with different colours: passive states lie in the black chamber, whereas maximally active states lie in the red one.
Let us first focus on the volume of the future thermal cone and analyse how it changes by increasing the inverse temperature
\beta
from
\beta=0
to
\beta\to\infty
. In the present analysis, the initial state is kept fixed, while the thermal state is taken to be a function of temperature
\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\boldsymbol{\gamma}(\beta)
, and it follows a trajectory from the centre of the probability simplex to the ground state (see Fig.
4
for an example considering a three-level system).

{marginfigure}
[0.1pt]

Thermal cone volumes
. For all permutations of the state
\boldsymbol{p}=(0.52,0.12,0.36)
, we plot the volume of the future thermal cone
\mathcal{V}^{\,\beta}_{+}
(top), the incomparable thermal region
\mathcal{V}^{\,\beta}_{\emptyset}
(centre) and the past thermal cone
\mathcal{V}^{\,\beta}_{-}
(bottom). Each colour corresponds to a permutation associated with a given chamber of the probability simplex. Among all states, two are distinct: the maximally active (red curve)
\boldsymbol{p}_{\text{max}}=(0.12,0.36,0.52)
and the passive (black curve)
\boldsymbol{p}_{p}=(0.52,0.36,0.12)
states. Any other permutation of the initial state characterises a different active state. The kinks in
\mathcal{V}^{\,\beta}_{+}
match with the inverse temperatures at which
\boldsymbol{p}
changes its
\beta
-ordering (vertical lines of matching colors). The three different simplices at the bottom show how the Weyl chambers change with
\beta
.

If at
\beta=0
the initial state
\boldsymbol{p}
is passive, the volume of its future thermal cone first decreases with
\beta
, and then starts to increase when
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
passes
\boldsymbol{p}
(i.e., when
\boldsymbol{p}
changes its
\beta
-ordering), tending asymptotically to a constant value (see black curves in Fig.
2
). However, if at
\beta=0
the initial state
\boldsymbol{p}
is maximally active, the behaviour of the volume of
\mathcal{T}_{+}^{\beta}(\boldsymbol{p})
differs from the previous case. As the thermal state approaches the ground state with increasing
\beta
, the distance between maximally active states and
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
increases with
\beta
, because the ground state and
\boldsymbol{p}
are located in opposite chambers. Consequently, the volume increases asymptotically to a constant value (see red curves in Fig.
2
). For general states, one can provide a qualitative explanation of the behaviour of their volumes based on their
\beta
-orderings. The inverse temperatures
\beta
for which one finds kinks in the future volume
\mathcal{V}_{+}^{\beta}(\boldsymbol{p})
(vertical lines in Fig.
2
) match with the transitions from a given
\beta
-ordering to another one. This should be compared with the isovolumetric level sets in Fig.
6
, where a matching non-smooth behaviour is found. Observe that similar kinks are not encountered for the past volume
\mathcal{V}^{\beta}_{-}(\boldsymbol{p})
, related to the smooth behaviour of the corresponding level sets. However, by considering a passive state
\boldsymbol{p}
and all its permutations, we can demonstrate that maximally active and passive states have maximum and minimum future volumes, respectively:

Figure 6:
Isovolumetric sets for thermal cones
. The volume of the future
\mathcal{V}^{\,\beta}_{+}
, incomparable
\mathcal{V}^{\,\beta}_{\emptyset}
, and past thermal regions
\mathcal{V}^{\,\beta}_{-}
, in the space of three-dimensional probability distributions for an equidistant energy spectrum
E_{1}=0
,
E_{2}=1
and
E_{3}=2
and inverse temperature (a)
\beta=0
and (b)
\beta=0.5
. The thermal state is depicted by a grey star
\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\bigstar
.

Proposition 3.2

(Max and min volumes)
.

For a
d
-dimensional energy-incoherent state
\boldsymbol{p}
with Hamiltonian
H
, and all states defined by permuting its population, the future thermal cone of the permutation resulting in the maximally active and passive states achieve maximum and minimum volumes, respectively.

Proof.

The Corollary is proven by noting that all permutations of
\boldsymbol{p}
are thermomajorised by the one corresponding to the maximally active state
\boldsymbol{p}_{\text{max}}
, while the associated passive state
\boldsymbol{p}_{p}
is thermomajorised by all the other permutations. Consequently,
\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p}_{p})\subset\mathcal{T}_{+}(\Pi\boldsymbol{p})
, while
\mathcal{T}_{+}(\Pi\boldsymbol{p})\subset\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p}_{\text{max}})
for any permutation matrix
\Pi
.
∎

Corollary
3.2
, also implies that the volume of the past thermal cone is minimum and maximum for the one corresponding to the maximally active and passive states, respectively (see Fig.
2
). To provide further characterisation of the volumes, we apply Lemma
2.5
to a non-full rank state, which allows us to derive the following result:

Proposition 3.3

(Zero volume)
.

The past thermal cone of a non-full rank state has volume zero despite being non-empty.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, consider a state of non-full rank
\boldsymbol{p}=(p_{1},...,p_{d-1},0)
. Applying Eq. (
17
) yields
\boldsymbol{t}^{(d,\boldsymbol{\pi})}=(1,0,...,0)
, for all
\boldsymbol{\pi}
. Consequently, the incomparable region is given by all points in the interior of the probability simplex, except those that are in the future of
\boldsymbol{p}
. Then, according to Theorem (
20
), all the points of the past will be located at the edge, and therefore the volume of
\mathcal{T}^{\,\beta}_{-}(\boldsymbol{p})
is zero.
∎

Understanding the behaviour of the thermal incomparable region is not directly straightforward. However, Corollary
3.3
helps us to find the state of non-full rank with the largest incomparable region:

Proposition 3.4

(Largest incomparagion region)
.

The non-full rank state with the largest thermal incomparable region is given by

\boldsymbol{g}=\frac{1}{Z}\left(e^{-\beta E_{1}},...,e^{-\beta E_{d-1}},0\right)\quad\text{where}\quad Z=\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}e^{-\beta E_{i}}.

(27)

Proof.

Consider an arbitrary non-full rank state
\boldsymbol{p}
. According to Corollary
3.3
, the volume of the thermal incomparable region can be written as
\mathcal{V}^{\,\beta}_{\emptyset}(\boldsymbol{p})=1-\mathcal{V}^{\,\beta}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p})
. Now note that,
\boldsymbol{p}\succ_{\beta}\boldsymbol{g}
, and
\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{g})\subset\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p})
. This implies that
\boldsymbol{g}
is the non-full rank state with the smallest future
thermal cone and, therefore, with the largest incomparable region.
∎

So far, the behaviour and properties of the volumes have been analysed and discussed without explicitly calculating them. There are several known algorithms for computing volumes of convex polytopes, such as triangulation, signed decomposition methods, or even direct integration
[
175
,
176
]
. These algorithms can be employed to obtain the volumes of past and future cones; the volume of the incomparable region can be calculated using the fact that the total volume of the probability simplex
\Delta_{d}
is equal to one. For a three-dimensional energy-incoherent state
\boldsymbol{p}
, expressions for the volumes can be easily derived. The starting point is to consider the Gauss area formula
[
177
]
, which allows us to determine the area of any polygon with vertices described by Cartesian coordinates. Taking into account a polygon
P
whose vertices, assumed to be arranged along the boundary in a clockwise manner, are denoted by
P_{i}=(x_{i},y_{i})
, with
i=\{1,...,n\}
, the Euclidean volume can be expressed as

V=\frac{1}{2}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{det}\begin{pmatrix}x_{i}&x_{i+1}\\
y_{i}&y_{i+1}\end{pmatrix}\right|,

(28)

where
x_{n+1}=x_{1}
and
y_{n+i}=y_{1}
. For
\beta=0
, deriving the volume of the thermal cones is straightforward, since the vertices, or extreme points, are permutation of
\boldsymbol{p}
. In this case, we arrive at the following closed-form expressions:

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\,0}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p})=

\displaystyle\>(3p^{\downarrow}_{1}-1)^{2}-3(p^{\downarrow}_{2}-p^{\downarrow}_{1})^{2},

(29)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\,0}_{\emptyset}(\boldsymbol{p})=

\displaystyle\>1-3(1-p^{\downarrow}_{1})^{2}+(1-3p^{\downarrow}_{3})^{2}-2\mathcal{V}^{\,0}_{+}(\boldsymbol{p})

(30)

\displaystyle\>+3\theta(1/2-p^{\downarrow}_{1})(1-2p^{\downarrow}_{1})^{2}

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\,0}_{-}(\boldsymbol{p})=

\displaystyle\>12p^{\downarrow}_{2}p^{\downarrow}_{3}-3\theta(1/2-p^{\downarrow}_{1})(1-2p^{\downarrow}_{1})^{2},

(31)

where
\theta
is the Heaviside step function. The situation involving a finite
\beta
is not as simple as before. Now, the extreme points are obtained by applying Lemma
2.2
, and, although computationally it is an easy task to calculate them, a neat and concise closed-form expression cannot be derived.

Finally, let us look at iso-volumetric curves for different values of
\beta
. In Fig.
6
, we plot these curves for a three-level system and four different temperatures. As expected, the symmetry is broken for any
\beta>0
as
E_{3}>E_{2}>E_{1}
. A simple fact worth mentioning is that the volume of the future thermal cone of the highest excited state
\boldsymbol{s}_{d}=(0,\ldots,0,1)
is always independent of
\beta
, maximum, and equal to unity. Conversely, the past thermal cone volume is maximum for a Gibbs state and equal to unity. Moreover, these curves give insight into how resourceful states are distributed within the space of states.

Subsection 3
The volumes of entanglement cones

Figure 7:
Isovolumetric sets for entanglement cones
. In order to calculate the isovolumetric lines for (a) the past cone, (b) the incomparable region and (c) the future cone for entanglement resource theory of states in
\mathcal{H}^{3\times 3}
one considers the density of the Schmidt coefficients
\boldsymbol{p}
induced by the Haar measure in the space
\mathcal{H}_{9}
of pure bipartite states, as depicted in panel (d) by showing a sample of
5\cdot 10^{4}
points in
\Delta_{3}
. Observe the scarcity of points in the central region, resulting in characteristic concentration of states with large future in the centre of the simplex and large past in the vicinity of the vertices.

Finally, we will briefly discuss the general qualitative aspects of the volumes of entanglement cones based on the numerical considerations. Detailed formal methods used in order to obtain them are discussed in Section
4
.

Naturally, depending on the context, the probability distribution
\boldsymbol{p}
may pertain to the Schmidt coefficients of a pure entangled state or the coefficients resulting from decomposing the state in a distinguished basis in the context of coherence
[
161
,
162
]
.
Despite the close connection between the resource theory of thermodynamics (at
\beta=0
) with the resource theories of entanglement and coherence, crucial differences appear already at the level of a single state as the order is reversed
[
160
]
, hence, interchanging the future
\mathcal{T}_{+}
with the past
\mathcal{T}_{-}
. The difference is even more pronounced within the context of volumes for the entanglement of pure states under LOCC operations.

The distribution of Schmidt coefficients induced by the uniform Haar measure in the space of pure bipartite states is significantly different from the flat distribution in the probability simplex
\Delta_{d}

[
178
]
. In particular, one observes a repulsion from the centre and, in the case of entangled systems of unequal dimension, from the facets of the simplex. Consequently, this implies a significant difference in the qualitative features of the isovolumetric curves. Fig.
7
shows the isovolumetric curves for
d=3
with equal-sized systems. Observe that states with large future volumes
\mathcal{V}_{+}
are concentrated around the centre of the simplex, which is explained by the repelling property. Inverse effects can be seen for the states with large past volumes
\mathcal{V}_{-}
, which concentrate at the boundaries of the simplex. The differences become even more pronounced for systems of unequal dimensions, as we will demonstrate in qualitative figures in Section
4
(see Fig.
10
).

Section 4
Coherent thermal cones for a two-level system

Figure 8:
For a two-level system with initial state
\rho
, represented by a black dot
\bullet
, and thermal state represented by a black star
\bigstar
with Bloch vectors
\boldsymbol{r}_{\rho}=(0.2,0,0.5)
and
\boldsymbol{r}_{\gamma}=(0,0,1/3)
, respectively, we depict in the real cross-section of the Bloch ball, the coherent thermal cone (a) under Gibbs-preserving operations (b) under thermal operations

Finally, let us now explain how to use the results from Refs.
[
179
]
and
[
153
]
to obtain the future and past thermal cones beyond energy-incoherent states. Specifically, we will explicitly construct the thermal cones for a qubit under thermal and Gibbs-preserving operations.

Coherent thermal cones for thermal operations

Consider initial and target states of a two level system,
\rho
and
\sigma
, with both written in the energy eigenbasis as

\rho=\begin{pmatrix}p&c\\
c&1-p\end{pmatrix}\quad,\quad\sigma=\begin{pmatrix}q&d\\
d&1-q\end{pmatrix},

(32)

where
c
and
d
are assumed to be real without loss of generality, which amounts to considering a cross-section of the Bloch ball in the
XZ
plane. Moreover, the thermal ground state occupation of the considered two-level system will be denoted by
\gamma
. It has been shown that for thermal operations, the coherences of the initial and target states have to satisfy the following inequality
[
179
]
,

d\leq c\frac{\sqrt{[q(1-\gamma)-\gamma(1-p)][p(1-\gamma)-\gamma(1-q)]}}{|p-\gamma|}.

(33)

Thus, we find the boundary of the future thermal cone by saturating Eq. (
33
), and solving it for
q
we obtain the achievable ground state occupation as a function of target coherence
d
,

\displaystyle q_{1}(d)

\displaystyle=\frac{(\gamma-p)\sqrt{c^{2}(1-2\gamma)^{2}+4\gamma d^{2}(1-\gamma)}}{2\gamma c(\gamma-1)}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{(p-\gamma)-2\gamma p(1-\gamma)}{2\gamma c(\gamma-1)}.

(34)

Therefore, the coherent future thermal cone is given by the region delimited by Eq. (
34
) from one side and a line segment connecting
(-c,p)
and
(c,p)
. To characterise the coherent past thermal cone, it will be convenient to introduce a number
d_{\text{cross}}\geq 0
defined by the relation
d_{\text{cross}}^{2}+q(d_{\text{cross}})^{2}=1
.

The coherent past thermal cone is generically composed of two disjoint regions. The first region is contained between a line segment connecting the points
(c,p)
and
(d_{\text{cross}},p)
, the curve
q_{1}(d)
for
d\in[c,d_{\text{cross}}]
and the boundary of the Bloch ball, together with its reflection with respect to the
Z
-axis.
The second one is obtained in a similar manner by focussing on the past state rather than the target, and thus by solving Eq. (
33
) with interchanges
p\leftrightarrow q
and
c\leftrightarrow d
. This results in

\displaystyle q_{2}(d)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\gamma c^{2}+\sqrt{c^{2}(p-\gamma)^{2}\left[(1-2\gamma)^{2}d^{2}-4c^{2}(\gamma-1)\gamma\right]}}{2\left[d^{2}+(\gamma-1)\gamma c^{2}\right]}

\displaystyle\>\>\>\>+\frac{d^{2}[p-\gamma-2\gamma p(1-\gamma)]}{2\left[d^{2}-(1-\gamma)\gamma c^{2}\right]},

(35)

with
d\in[d_{\textrm{min}},d_{\textrm{max}}]
, where
d_{\textrm{min}}
and
d_{\textrm{max}}
are real positive solutions of equation
q(d)^{2}+d^{2}=1
, such that
d_{\text{cross}}\leq d_{\text{min}}\leq d_{\text{max}}
. However, for large values of coherence
c
, we note that this second region may not appear at all. Finally, the incomparable region
\mathcal{T}_{\emptyset}(\rho)
is obtained by subtracting the past and future cones from the entire Bloch ball.

Coherent thermal cones for Gibbs-preserving operations

Consider a parametrisation of qubit states
\rho
in the Bloch sphere representation,

\rho=\frac{\mathbbm{1}+\boldsymbol{r}_{\rho}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}}{2},

(36)

where
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{x},\sigma_{y},\sigma_{z})
denotes the vector of Pauli matrices. The Bloch vectors of the starting state
\rho
, target state
\rho^{\prime}
and the Gibbs state
\gamma
are given by:

\boldsymbol{r}_{\rho}=(x,y,z),\quad\boldsymbol{r}_{\rho^{\prime}}=(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime}),\quad\boldsymbol{r}_{\gamma}=(0,0,\zeta),

(37)

where the
z
coordinate of the Gibbs state can be related to the partition function
Z
by
\zeta=2Z^{-1}-1\geq 0
.

According to Ref.
[
153
]
, there exists a GP quantum channel
\mathcal{E}
such that
\mathcal{E}(\rho)=\rho^{\prime}
if and only if
R_{\pm}(\rho)\geq R_{\pm}(\rho^{\prime})
for both signs, where
R_{\pm}(\rho)=\delta(\rho)\pm\zeta z
and

\delta(\rho):=\sqrt{(z-\zeta)^{2}+(x^{2}+y^{2})(1-\zeta^{2})}.

(38)

Consequently, the future thermal cone
\mathcal{T}_{+}(\rho)
of any qubit state
\rho
under GP operations can be directly constructed from the above result. For a generic qubit state
\rho
, we first orient the Bloch sphere so that its
XZ
plane coincides with the plane containing
\rho
and a thermal state
\gamma
, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{r}_{\rho}=(x,0,z)
. Then, define two disks,
D_{1}(\rho)
and
D_{2}(\rho)
with corresponding circles
C_{1}(\rho)
and
C_{2}(\rho)
, of radii

R_{1}(\rho)=\frac{R_{-}(\rho)+\zeta^{2}}{1-\zeta^{2}},\quad R_{2}(\rho)=\frac{R_{+}(\rho)-\zeta^{2}}{1-\zeta^{2}},

(39)

centred at

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\boldsymbol{z}_{1}(\rho)&=&[0,0,\zeta(1+R_{1}(\rho))],\\
\boldsymbol{z}_{2}(\rho)&=&[0,0,\zeta(1-R_{2}(\rho))].\end{array}

(40)

Therefore, the future thermal cone under GP quantum channels is given by the intersection of two disks of radii
R_{1}(\rho)
and
R_{2}(\rho)
centred at
\boldsymbol{z}_{1}(\rho)
and
\boldsymbol{z}_{2}(\rho)
,
\mathcal{T}_{+}(\rho)=D_{1}(\rho)\cap D_{2}(\rho)
.

The incomparable region is given by mixed conditions, i.e.,
\rho^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{\emptyset}(\rho)
if and only if
R_{\pm}(\rho)\geq R_{\pm}(\rho^{\prime})
and
R_{\mp}(\rho)<R_{\mp}(\rho^{\prime})
, or in terms of the disks given beforehand,
\mathcal{T}_{\emptyset}(\rho)=D_{1}(\rho)\cap D_{2}(\rho)\backslash\mathcal{T}_{+}(\rho)
. Finally, the past cone
\mathcal{T}_{-}(\rho)
can be easily given by subtracting the future cone and the incomparable region from the entire Bloch ball.

Section 5
Derivation of the results

In this section, we derive the two most important results of this chapter, namely, the incomparable thermal region for
\beta=0
and
\beta\geq 0
. We proceed by first considering the case of
\beta=0
, covered by Lemma
1.2
, and develop the notion of the so-called tangent vectors
\boldsymbol{t}^{(n)}
that comprise the boundary of the incomparable region. Then, we generalize the notion of tangent vectors and use it in the proof of Lemma
2.5
, which concerns the incomparable region for
\beta>0
.

Subsection 1
Infinite-temperature

a. Tangent vector

The first concept we will need in the proof is that of a
tangent probability vector
, referred to as the tangent vector for brevity, which will prove to be an essential ingredient.

Let
\Delta_{d}
be the set of probability vectors of dimension
d
with real entries,
\Delta_{d}=\left\{(p_{1},...,p_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\sum_{i}p_{i}=1\right\}
, and let us restrict ourselves to vectors ordered in a non-increasing order, i.e.,
p_{i}\geq p_{i+1}
. To avoid complications, we assume, without loss of generality, that all
p_{i}\neq p_{j}
. Following the notation established in Chapter
2
, we will denote probability vectors by bold lowercase letters
\boldsymbol{p}\in\Delta_{d}
and their corresponding cumulative counterparts by bold uppercase vectors
\boldsymbol{p}:P_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{i}p_{i}
with
i\in{0,\ldots,d}
. For any vector
\boldsymbol{p}
, we introduce a tangent vector
\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{p})\equiv\boldsymbol{t}\in\Delta_{d}
by imposing that all its components except the first and the last are equal,
t_{i}=t_{j}
for all
1<i<j<d
. Additionally, we require that the cumulative vector
\boldsymbol{T}
agrees with the vector
\boldsymbol{p}
in at most two consecutive points, i.e.,
T_{i}=P_{i}
and
T_{j}>P_{j}
for all
j\in\left\{1,\ldots,d-1\right\}\backslash{i}
, or
T_{j}=P_{j}
for
j\in\left\{i,i+1\right\}
and
T_{j}>P_{j}
elsewhere. The two imposed conditions follow the intuition of tangency and, by construction, satisfy the majorisation relation
\boldsymbol{t}\prec\boldsymbol{p}
. Furthermore, note that
T_{0}=P_{0}=0
and
T_{d}=P_{d}=1
and thus are naturally excluded from the considerations.

Figure 9:

Lorenz curves of the tangent vectors
\boldsymbol{t}^{(n)}
. Majorisation curves of the state
\boldsymbol{p}=(0.43,0.37,0.18,0.02)
(black) and (a)
\boldsymbol{t}^{(1)}
(b)
\boldsymbol{t}^{(2)}
(c)
\boldsymbol{t}^{(3)}
(d)
\boldsymbol{t}^{(4)}
and (e) all tangent vectors
j\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, respectively.

Indeed, assuming equality between
\boldsymbol{T}
and
\boldsymbol{P}
at exactly two consecutive points restricts the tangent vectors to a set of
d
unique probability vectors
\boldsymbol{t}^{(n)}
defined as

\boldsymbol{t}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{p})\equiv\boldsymbol{t}^{(n)}=\left(t^{(n)}_{1},p_{n},...,p_{n},t^{(n)}_{d}\right),

(41)

for
1\leq n\leq d
with the first and last components given by

t_{1}^{(n)}=\sum_{i}^{n-1}p_{i}-(n-2)p_{n}\quad,\quad t_{d}^{(n)}=1-t^{(n)}_{1}-(d-2)p_{n}.

(42)

Observe that the tangent vectors
\boldsymbol{t}^{(n)}
that agree with the Lorenz curve of
\boldsymbol{p}
at two successive points can be used to construct all possible tangent vectors
\boldsymbol{t}
that satisfy the condition of agreement at at least a single point,
T_{i}=P_{i}
. Indeed, consider a vector
\boldsymbol{t}(\lambda,i)\equiv\boldsymbol{t}=(1-\lambda)\boldsymbol{t}^{(i)}+\lambda\boldsymbol{t}^{(i+1)}
. Direct calculation shows that it is tangent at just one point,
T_{i}=\lambda T_{i}^{(i)}+(1-\lambda)T_{i}^{(i+1)}=P_{i}
. Similarly, we arrive at
T_{j}>P_{j}
for
j\neq i
and
0<\lambda<1
.

Thus, starting with a discrete set of
d
tangent vectors
\boldsymbol{t}^{(n)}
, we recover the entire continuous family of tangent vectors
\boldsymbol{t}(\lambda,i)
. This argument can be further formalised by considering the Lorenz curves
f_{\boldsymbol{p}}(x)
and
f_{\boldsymbol{t}}(x)
. Assuming the left and right derivatives of the former,
\lim_{x\rightarrow\frac{i}{d}-}f^{\prime}{\boldsymbol{p}}(x)=d\cdot p_{i}
and
\lim_{x\rightarrow\frac{i}{d}+}f^{\prime}{\boldsymbol{p}}(x)=d\cdot p_{i+1}
, we obtain the extremal slope values for the tangent lines at the
i
-th elbow. Now, considering the second Lorenz curve by construction we have that
f_{\boldsymbol{t}}(i/d)=f_{\boldsymbol{p}}(i/d)
and its derivative at this point,
f^{\prime}{\boldsymbol{t}}(i/d)=d\left[(1-\lambda)p_{i}+\lambda p{i+1}\right]
, span all values between the extremal slope values
d\cdot p_{i}
and
d\cdot p_{i+1}
, therefore exhausting the family of possible tangent lines at the
i
-th elbow.

b. Lattice

Lattices provide a setting in which it is natural to represent the precedence or succession of elements within a given set. In particular, they can be used to establish a time-like structure for a given set, as defined by a lattice (see Definition
2.1
).

It is well known that the partially ordered set
(\Delta_{d},\succ)
of
d
-dimensional probability vectors with real entries in non-increasing order under majorisation forms a lattice
[
180
,
153
]
. In this setting, the join
\boldsymbol{p}\vee\boldsymbol{q}
can be interpreted as the last common past point of
\boldsymbol{p}
and
\boldsymbol{q}
, while the meet
\boldsymbol{p}\wedge\boldsymbol{q}
can be seen as the first common future point of the pair
\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{q}
. The procedure to obtain the join and meet has been illustrated in Ref.
[
153
]
, and since part of our proof relies on the existence of the join, we will now review the algorithm used to construct it.

To construct the join of
\boldsymbol{p}
and
\boldsymbol{q}
, we start with a probability vector
\boldsymbol{r}^{(0)}
with elements defined by

r_{i}^{(0)}=\max\left\{\boldsymbol{P}_{i},\boldsymbol{Q}_{i}\right\}-\max\left\{\boldsymbol{P}_{i-1},\boldsymbol{Q}_{i-1}\right\}.

(43)

At this stage, it is possible that the entries of
\boldsymbol{r}^{(0)}
are not ordered in a non-increasing manner. However, we can obtain a properly ordered probability vector
\boldsymbol{r}=\boldsymbol{p}\vee\boldsymbol{q}
, defining the actual join, in no more than
d-1
steps. In each step
k\geq 0
, we define
N\geq 2
as the smallest index where there is an increase between two consecutive components of the probability vector
r^{(k)}
, i.e.,
r^{(k)}N>r^{(k)}{N-1}
. Next, we define
M\leq N-1
in a way that introducing constant probabilities for the entries with
i\in{M,\ldots,N}
eliminates the growth. This is done by ensuring that

r^{(k)}_{M}\geq\frac{\sum_{i=M}^{N}r_{i}^{(k)}}{N-M+1}=:a_{k}.

(44)

Thus, the next iterative step
\boldsymbol{r}^{(k+1)}
is defined by setting its components as

r_{i}^{(k+1)}=\begin{cases}a_{k}&\text{for }i\in\{M,\ldots,N\}\\
r_{i}^{(k)}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

(45)

This construction is repeated until for some
k^{\prime}
the probability vector
\boldsymbol{r}^{(k^{\prime})}\equiv\boldsymbol{r}
is ordered non-increasingly; in this way we get the proper join.

c. Incomparable region and the boundaries

As a final piece of information needed to understand the proofs, we introduce the definition of the boundary of the past cone.

Definition 5.1

(Boundary of the past thermal cone)
.

Consider a
d
-dimensional energy incoherent state
\boldsymbol{p}\in\mathcal{P}_{d}
with
d\geq 3
. We define the boundary of the past thermal cone as the set of probability vectors
\boldsymbol{q}\succ\boldsymbol{p}
for which the cumulative vector
\boldsymbol{Q}
is equal to the cumulative vector
\boldsymbol{P}
at least one point. In other words,
\boldsymbol{P}_{j}<\boldsymbol{Q}_{j}
for some proper subset of the indices
j
and
\boldsymbol{P}_{i}=\boldsymbol{Q}_{i}
for all other indices.

One can define the boundary of the future cone in a similar manner by changing the direction of the inequalities. By considering the common part of the boundary between the future and past cones, we arrive at a simple observation:

Observation 5.1

(Common point of future and past cones)
.

A point that lies simultaneously at the common part of the boundary between the future and the past must fulfil
\forall_{i}P_{i}=Q_{i}
. Therefore, for
\beta=0
we have the equality of
\boldsymbol{p}
and
\boldsymbol{q}
up to a permutation, giving a total of
d!
common points between the future and the past of any vector
\boldsymbol{p}
.

Equipped with the notion of tangent vectors
\boldsymbol{t}^{(n)}
, the join
\boldsymbol{p}\vee\boldsymbol{q}
and the boundary of the past cone, we are now prepared to tackle the Lemma
1.2
concerning the incomparable region, and this is done by proving the following result:

Lemma 5.2
.

Consider
\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{q}\in\Delta_{d}
and assume that
\boldsymbol{p}\nsucc\boldsymbol{q}
. Then,
\boldsymbol{q}
belongs to the incomparable region of
\boldsymbol{p}
,
\boldsymbol{q}\in\mathcal{T}_{\emptyset}(\boldsymbol{p})
, if and only if it belongs to the future majorisation cone of some vector
\boldsymbol{t}
tangent to
\boldsymbol{p}
,
\boldsymbol{q}\in\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{t})
, with

\boldsymbol{t}\equiv\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{p};\lambda,n)=\lambda\boldsymbol{t}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{p})+(1-\lambda)\boldsymbol{t}^{(n+1)}(\boldsymbol{p}),

for some
n\in\{1,d-1\}
and
\lambda\in[0,1]
.

Proof.

To prove the “if” direction, we take a probability vector lying in the interior of the future of the tangent vector,
\boldsymbol{q}\in\operatorname{int}[\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{t})]
. Then, to prove that
\boldsymbol{q}\in\mathcal{T}_{\emptyset}(\boldsymbol{p})
, one needs to show that
\boldsymbol{q}\nsucc\boldsymbol{p}
. By construction, we have
T_{k}\geq P_{k}
, for every
k\neq n
, with equality when
k=n
, so
P_{n}=T_{n}>Q_{n}
with the equality excluded by putting
\boldsymbol{q}
in the interior of the future. Consequently,
P_{n}>Q_{n}
, and thus
\boldsymbol{q}\nsucc\boldsymbol{p}
and by the initial assumption,
\boldsymbol{p}\nsucc\boldsymbol{q}
. Therefore,
\boldsymbol{q}\in\mathcal{T}_{\emptyset}(\boldsymbol{p})
.

In order to demonstrate the “only if” direction, let us take an arbitrary
\boldsymbol{q}\in\mathcal{T}_{\emptyset}(\boldsymbol{p})
and recall that there always exists the last common past point for
\boldsymbol{p}
and
\boldsymbol{q}
called join,
\boldsymbol{r}=\boldsymbol{p}\vee\boldsymbol{q}
. From the construction of the join
\boldsymbol{r}
, it is found that the entries of the cumulative distribution
\boldsymbol{R}
will be divided into three subsets, namely points common with
\boldsymbol{P}
, common with
\boldsymbol{Q}
and the ones lying above either, that is
I_{p}:=\left\{i:0<i<d,\,R_{i}=P_{i}\right\}
,
I_{q}:=\left\{i:0<i<d,\,R_{i}=Q_{i}\right\}
and
J:=\left\{0<j<d,\,:R_{j}>\max(P_{j},Q_{j})\right\}
, respectively. In particular, looking at equation (
45
) one can see that it is either the case that
M-1\in I_{p}
and
N+1\in I_{q}
, or
M-1\in I_{p}
and
N+1\in I_{q}
or by invoking geometric intuition, endpoints of the flat fragments of
\boldsymbol{R}
will join
\boldsymbol{P}
and
\boldsymbol{Q}
. Thus, at each step, the sets
I_{p}
and
I_{q}
will be non-empty.
By this argument, we may choose any index
i\in I_{p}
and construct a tangent vector
\boldsymbol{t}^{\prime}
for the join
\boldsymbol{r}
at the
i
-th elbow.

\boldsymbol{t}^{\prime}\equiv\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{r};\mu,i)=\mu\boldsymbol{t}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{r})+(1-\mu)\boldsymbol{t}^{(i+1)}(\boldsymbol{r})

(46)

for any
\mu\in[0,1]
. This vector obeys, by construction, the majorisation relation
\boldsymbol{t}^{\prime}\succ\boldsymbol{r}\succ\boldsymbol{q}
. Furthermore, due to the choice
i\in I_{p}
it is also a tangent vector for the
\boldsymbol{p}
,

\boldsymbol{t}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{t}\equiv\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{p};\lambda(\mu),i)=\lambda(\mu)\boldsymbol{t}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{p})+(1-\lambda(\mu))\boldsymbol{t}^{(i+1)}(\boldsymbol{p})

(47)

for
\lambda(\mu)\in[0,1]
. Therefore, by the properties of the tangent vector
\boldsymbol{t}
it follows that
\boldsymbol{q}\in\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{t})
.
∎

The last step necessary in order to demonstrate the Lemma
1.2
is to notice that the tangent vectors
\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{p};\lambda(\mu),i)
are convex combinations of
\boldsymbol{t}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{p})
and
\boldsymbol{t}^{(i+1)}(\boldsymbol{p})
and their future majorisation cones are convex, therefore, union of their future cones corresponds to the convex hull of the future cones of the extreme points

\bigcup_{\lambda\in[0,1]}\mathcal{T}_{+}(\boldsymbol{t}(\boldsymbol{p};\lambda,i))=\text{conv}\left[\mathcal{T}_{+}\left(\boldsymbol{t}^{(i)}\right)\cup\mathcal{T}_{+}\left(\boldsymbol{t}^{(i+1)}\right)\right]

(48)

which completes the statement of Lemma
1.2
.∎

Subsection 2
Finite temperatures

The proof of Lemma
2.5
for
\beta>0
is developed in the simplest way by considering the embedding
\mathfrak{M}
introduced in Section
1
, which takes
d
-dimensional probability distributions
\boldsymbol{p}\in\Delta_{d}
to its higher-dimensional image
\mathfrak{M}(\boldsymbol{p})\in\Delta^{\mathfrak{M}}_{d}

which allows us to closely follow the steps of the proof for
\beta=0
.

After shifting our focus to the embedded space, we construct the corresponding tangent vectors
\boldsymbol{t}^{\mathfrak{M}}(\boldsymbol{p};\lambda,n)=\lambda\boldsymbol{t}^{\mathfrak{M}(n)}(\boldsymbol{p})+(1-\lambda)\boldsymbol{t}^{\mathfrak{M}(n+1)}(\boldsymbol{p})
that respect the rules for constructing the majorisation curve within the embedded space. In particular, in full analogy to the
\beta=0
case, the full family can be given in terms of vectors tangent to the
n
-th linear fragment of the embedded majorisation curve,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}^{\mathfrak{M}(n)}(\boldsymbol{p})=\left(t_{1}^{\mathfrak{M}(n)},\frac{p_{n}}{\gamma_{n}}\gamma^{\mathfrak{M}}_{2},\ldots,\frac{p_{n}}{\gamma_{n}}\gamma^{\mathfrak{M}}_{2^{d}-1},t_{2^{d}}^{\mathfrak{M}(n)}\right),

(49)

where the first entry is defined in such a way that the majorisation curves, defined as the piecewise-linear functions given by their elbows
\left\{\left(\Gamma^{\mathfrak{M}}_{i},P^{\mathfrak{M}}_{i}\right)\right\}_{i=0}^{2d-1}
, agree in at least one point,
f^{\mathfrak{M}}_{\boldsymbol{t}^{(n)}}(\Gamma^{\mathfrak{M}}_{n})=f_{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\mathfrak{M}}(\Gamma^{\mathfrak{M}}_{n})
and the last one guarantees that
\sum_{i}t_{i}^{\mathfrak{M}(n)}=1
. Observe that vectors
\boldsymbol{t}^{\mathfrak{M}(n)}
constructed in this way are tangent with respect to the embedded Lorenz curve, i.e.,
t^{\mathfrak{M}(n)}_{i}/\gamma^{\mathfrak{M}}_{i}=t^{\mathfrak{M}(n)}_{j}/\gamma^{\mathfrak{M}}_{j}
, thus taking into account the varying intervals on the horizontal axis. Equipped with these, we pose a technical lemma similar to Lemma
5.2
,

As a preliminary step, we give the algorithm for the construction of the join in the embedding space by modifying the crucial steps (
44
) and (
45
) to take into account the varying widths and redefine the point
N
of increase by requiring
r_{N}^{\mathfrak{M}(k)}/\gamma_{N}^{\mathfrak{M}}>r_{N-1}^{\mathfrak{M}(k)}/\gamma_{N-1}^{\mathfrak{M}}
. Consequently, we redefine
M
by a condition similar to (
44
) that incorporates the scaling,

\displaystyle\frac{r^{\mathfrak{M}(k)}_{M}}{\gamma^{\mathfrak{M}}_{M}}\geq\frac{\sum_{i=M}^{N}r_{i}^{\mathfrak{M}(k)}}{\sum_{i=M}^{N}\gamma_{i}^{\mathfrak{M}}}=:a^{\mathfrak{M}}_{k}.

(50)

Finally, we define the join candidate in the
k
-th step in full analogy to (
45
) as

r_{i}^{\mathfrak{M}(k+1)}=\begin{cases}a_{k}^{\mathfrak{M}}\gamma_{i}^{\mathfrak{M}}&\text{for }i\in\{M,\ldots,N\}\\
r_{i}^{\mathfrak{M}(k)}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

(51)

The algorithm defined in this way follows precisely the same logic as the one given in Ref.
[
153
]
and thus it always terminates, in this in no more than
d^{\mathfrak{M}}(\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{q})-1=\overline{\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{j}\gamma_{\boldsymbol{\pi}^{-1}_{\boldsymbol{p}}(i)}\right\}_{j=1}^{d}\cup\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{j}\gamma_{\boldsymbol{\pi}^{-1}_{\boldsymbol{q}}(i)}\right\}_{j=1}^{d}}-1
steps.

As a final remark, one has to note that the family of tangent vectors
\boldsymbol{t}^{\mathfrak{M}(n)}(\boldsymbol{r}^{\mathfrak{M}})
should be indexed by
n\in\{1,\ldots,d^{\prime}\}
, where the number
d^{\prime}
of constant-slope fragments of the join, even though bounded,
d^{\mathfrak{M}}(\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{q})\geq d^{\prime}\geq d
, is
a priori
not well defined due to many possible ways of disagreement between the
\beta
-orders of
\boldsymbol{p}
and
\boldsymbol{q}
. With these tools, we are ready to present the technical lemma needed for constructing the incomparable region for
\beta>0
.

Lemma 5.3
.

\mathfrak{M}(\boldsymbol{q})\equiv\boldsymbol{q}^{\mathfrak{M}}
belongs to the incomparable region of
\mathfrak{M}(\boldsymbol{p})\equiv\boldsymbol{p}^{\mathfrak{M}}
,
\boldsymbol{q}^{\mathfrak{M}}\nsucc_{\mathfrak{M}}\boldsymbol{p}^{\mathfrak{M}}
