so that
\epsilon_{\mathrm{d}}=\epsilon
.

Using the communication protocol described above, we then get the following asymptotic lower bound on the optimal thermodynamically-free encoding rate into a state
\rho^{N}
[recall Eq. (
36
)]
\sidenote
The optimal encoding rate is given by:

R(\rho^{N},\epsilon_{\mathrm{d}}):=\frac{\log[M(\rho^{N},\epsilon_{\mathrm{d}})]}{N}\,.

:

R(\rho^{N},\epsilon_{\mathrm{d}})\geq\frac{\beta}{N}(F^{N}+\sigma(F^{N})\Phi^{-1}(\epsilon_{\mathrm{d}}))+o\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\right).

(32)

The above lower bound is exactly matching the upper bound for
R(\rho^{N},\epsilon_{\mathrm{d}})
recently derived in Ref.
[
141
]
for a slightly different scenario with
\rho^{N}_{n}=\rho
and
H^{N}_{n}=H
for all
n
, with
\tilde{H}^{N}=H^{N}
, and with Gibbs-preserving operation instead of thermal operations. However, the proof presented there can be easily adapted to work in the current case if we keep the first restriction, i.e., when the initial state is
\rho^{N}=\rho^{\otimes N}
and all initial subsystems have equal Hamiltonians. We explain in detail how to adapt that proof in Section
6
, where we also explain what technical result concerning hypothesis testing relative entropy needs to be proven in order to make the proof also work when subsystems are not identical. Here we conclude that

\!\!\!R(\rho^{\otimes N},\epsilon_{\mathrm{d}})\!=\!D(\rho\|\gamma)+\frac{\sqrt{V(\rho\|\gamma)}}{\sqrt{N}}\Phi^{-1}(\epsilon_{\mathrm{d}})+o\!\left(\!\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\!\right)\!,\!

(33)

where
\rho
is either a pure or incoherent state.

The above result can be thermodynamically interpreted as the inverse of the Szilard engine. While the Szilard engine converts bits of information into work, the protocol studied here employs the free energy of the system (i.e., the ability to perform work) to encode bits of information. While the asymptotic result was recently proven in Ref.
[
140
]
, here we proved that this relation is deeper as it also connects fluctuations of free energy to the optimal average decoding error.

Section 5
Derivation of the results

In what follows, we first introduce the mathematical formalism used to study the incoherent distillation process. We then use it to prove Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
. Finally, we also prove Theorems
3.3
and
3.4
by first mapping the problem of distillation from pure states to an equivalent incoherent problem, and then using the formalism of incoherent distillations.

Subsection 1
Incoherent distillation process

Distillation conditions via approximate majorisation

We begin by restating the crucial theorem based on Ref.
[
57
]
regarding thermodynamic interconversion for incoherent states.

Theorem 5.1

(Approximate thermomajorisation)
.

For the initial and target system with the same thermal distribution
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
, there exists a thermal operation mapping between an energy-incoherent state
\boldsymbol{p}
and a state
\epsilon
-close to
\boldsymbol{q}
in infidelity distance, if and only if
\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}\succ_{\epsilon}\hat{\boldsymbol{q}}
.

Despite the fact that in our case we want to study the general case of initial and final systems with different Hamiltonians, with a little bit of ingenuity we can still use the above theorem. Namely, we consider a family of total systems composed of the first
N
subsystems with initial Hamiltonians
H^{N}_{n}
, and the remaining part described by the target Hamiltonian
\tilde{H}^{N}
. We choose initial states of the total system on the first
N
subsystems to be a general product of incoherent states
\boldsymbol{p}^{N}_{n}
, while the remaining part to be prepared in a thermal equilibrium state
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{N}
corresponding to
\tilde{H}^{N}
. Since Gibbs states are free, this setting is thermodynamically equivalent to having just the first
N
systems with Hamiltonians
H^{N}_{n}
and in states
\boldsymbol{p}_{n}^{N}
. Moreover, for target states of the total system, we choose thermal equilibrium states
\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{N}_{n}
for the first
N
subsystems, and sharp states
\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{k}^{N}
of the Hamiltonian
\tilde{H}^{N}
for the remaining part. Again, this is thermodynamically equivalent to having just the system with Hamiltonian
\tilde{H}^{N}
and in a state
\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{k}^{N}
. Thus, employing Theorem
5.1
, an
\epsilon
-approximate distillation process for incoherent states exists if and only if:

\left(\bigotimes_{n=1}^{N}\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}^{N}_{n}\otimes\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}^{N}\right)\succ_{\epsilon}\left(\bigotimes_{n=1}^{N}\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{N}_{n}\otimes\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}}_{k}^{N}\right).

(34)

This way, using a single fixed Hamiltonian, we can encode transformations between different Hamiltonians.

Let us introduce the following shorthand notation:

\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}:=\bigotimes_{n=1}^{N}\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}^{N}_{n},\qquad\hat{\boldsymbol{G}}^{N}:=\bigotimes_{n=1}^{N}{\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}^{N}_{n}=\bigotimes_{n=1}^{N}{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{N}_{n}.

(35)

Then, we can use the previous facts on the embedding map to conclude with the following statement: there exists an
\epsilon
-approximate thermodynamic distillation process from
N
systems with Hamiltonians
H^{N}_{n}
and in energy-incoherent states
\boldsymbol{p}^{N}_{n}
to a system with a Hamiltonian
\tilde{H}^{N}
and in a sharp energy eigenstate
\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{k}^{N}
if and only if

\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}\otimes\tilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{N}\succ_{\epsilon}\hat{\boldsymbol{G}}^{N}\otimes\tilde{\boldsymbol{f}}^{N}_{k}.

(36)

Information-theoretic intermission

Before we proceed, we need to make a short intermission for a few important comments concerning information-theoretic quantities introduced in Section
1
. For incoherent states
\rho
and
\gamma
represented by probability vectors
\boldsymbol{p}
and
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
, these simplify and take the following classical form:

\displaystyle D(\boldsymbol{p}\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}):=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}p_{i}\left(\log\frac{p_{i}}{\gamma_{i}}\right),

(37a)

\displaystyle V(\boldsymbol{p}\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}):=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}p_{i}\left(\log\frac{p_{i}}{\gamma_{i}}-D(\boldsymbol{p}\|\boldsymbol{\gamma})\right)^{2},

(37b)

\displaystyle Y(\boldsymbol{p}\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}):=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}p_{i}\left|\log\frac{p_{i}}{\gamma_{i}}-D(\boldsymbol{p}\|\boldsymbol{\gamma})\right|^{3}.

(37c)

Moreover, by direct calculation, one can easily show that the above quantities are invariant under embedding, i.e.,
D(\boldsymbol{p}\|\boldsymbol{\gamma})=D(\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}\|\boldsymbol{\eta})
, and the same holds for
V
and
Y
. Therefore

\displaystyle D(\boldsymbol{p}\|\boldsymbol{\gamma})=

\displaystyle D(\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}\|\boldsymbol{\eta})=\log D-H(\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}),

(38a)

\displaystyle V(\boldsymbol{p}\|\boldsymbol{\gamma})=

\displaystyle V(\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}\|\boldsymbol{\eta})=V(\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}),

(38b)

\displaystyle Y(\boldsymbol{p}\|\boldsymbol{\gamma})=

\displaystyle Y(\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}\|\boldsymbol{\eta})=Y(\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}),

(38c)

where

\displaystyle H(\boldsymbol{p}):=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}p_{i}(-\log p_{i}),

(39a)

\displaystyle V(\boldsymbol{p}):=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}p_{i}(\log p_{i}+H(\boldsymbol{p}))^{2},

(39b)

\displaystyle Y(\boldsymbol{p}):=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}p_{i}\left|\log p_{i}+H(\boldsymbol{p})\right|^{3},

(39c)

and note that
V(\boldsymbol{p})=0
if and only if
\boldsymbol{p}
is a flat state.

Optimal error for a distillation process

In order to transform the approximate majorisation condition from Eq. (
36
) into an explicit expression for the optimal transformation error, we start from the following result proven by the authors of Ref.
[
103
]
.

Lemma 5.2

(Optimal transformation error)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{p}
and
\boldsymbol{q}
be distributions with
V(\boldsymbol{q})=0
. Then

\displaystyle\min\left\{\epsilon\middle|\boldsymbol{p}\succ_{\epsilon}\boldsymbol{q}\right\}=1-\sum_{i=1}^{\exp H(\boldsymbol{q})}p_{i}^{\downarrow}.

(40)

Applying the above lemma to Eq. (
36
) yields the following expression for the optimal error
\epsilon_{N}
:

\epsilon_{N}=1-\sum_{i=1}^{\exp[H(\hat{\boldsymbol{G}}^{N})+H(\tilde{\boldsymbol{f}}_{k}^{N})]}\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}\otimes\tilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{N}\right)_{i}^{\downarrow}.

(41)

Now, for an arbitrary distribution
\boldsymbol{p}
and any flat state
\boldsymbol{f}
, we make two observations: the size of the support of
\boldsymbol{f}
is simply
\exp(H(\v{f}))
, and the entries of
\boldsymbol{p}\otimes\boldsymbol{f}
are just the copied and scaled entries of
\boldsymbol{p}
. As a result, the sum of the
l
largest elements of
\boldsymbol{p}
can be expressed as

\sum_{i=1}^{l}p_{i}^{\downarrow}=\sum_{i=1}^{l\exp(H(\v{f}))}(\boldsymbol{p}\otimes\boldsymbol{f})_{i}^{\downarrow}.

(42)

Inverting the above expression we can write

\sum_{i=1}^{l}(\boldsymbol{p}\otimes\boldsymbol{f})_{i}^{\downarrow}=\sum_{i=1}^{l\exp(-H(\v{f}))}p_{i}^{\downarrow},

(43)

where the summation with non-integer upper limit
x
should be interpreted as:

\sum_{i=1}^{x}p_{i}:=\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor x\rfloor}p_{i}+(x-\lfloor x\rfloor)p_{\lceil x\rceil}.

(44)

Since
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
is a flat state, we conclude that

\epsilon_{N}=1-\sum_{i=1}^{\exp[H(\hat{\boldsymbol{G}}^{N})+H(\tilde{\boldsymbol{f}}_{k}^{N})-H(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{N})]}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}^{\downarrow}.

(45)

We see that the error depends crucially on partial ordered sums as above. To deal with these kind of sums, we introduce the function
\chi_{\boldsymbol{p}}
defined implicitly by the following equation

\sum_{i=1}^{\chi_{\boldsymbol{p}}(l)}p_{i}^{\downarrow}=\sum_{i}\{p_{i}|p_{i}\geq 1/l\}.

(46)

In words:
\chi_{\boldsymbol{p}}(l)
counts the number of entries of
\boldsymbol{p}
that are larger than
1/l
. Now, we have the following lemma that will be crucial in proving our theorems.

Lemma 5.3

(Ordered summation bounds)
.

Every
d
-dimensional probability distribution
\boldsymbol{p}
satisfies the following for all
l\in\{1,\dots,d\}
and for all
\alpha\geq 1
:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{l}p_{i}^{\downarrow}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{\chi_{\boldsymbol{p}}(l)}p_{i}^{\downarrow},

(47a)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{l}p_{i}^{\downarrow}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\chi_{\boldsymbol{p}}(\alpha l)/c}p_{i}^{\downarrow},

(47b)

where

c=\sqrt{\alpha}\sum_{i=\chi_{\boldsymbol{p}}(\sqrt{\alpha}l)}^{\chi_{\boldsymbol{p}}(\alpha l)}p_{i}^{\downarrow}.

(48)

Moreover, as the probabilities are ordered in the sums given in Eq. (
47a
) and Eq. (
47b
), it simply follows that

\chi_{\boldsymbol{p}}(l)\leq l\leq\chi_{\boldsymbol{p}}(\alpha l)/c.

(49)

Proof.

The first inequality is very easily proven by observing that the number of entries larger than
1/l
, i.e.,
\chi_{\boldsymbol{p}}(l)
, is bounded from above by
l
due to normalisation. Now, to prove the second inequality, we start from the following observation:

\sum_{i=1}^{\chi_{\boldsymbol{p}}(\sqrt{\alpha}l)}\left(p_{i}^{\downarrow}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha}l}\right)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{\chi_{\boldsymbol{p}}(\alpha l)}\left(p_{i}^{\downarrow}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha}l}\right),

(50)

which comes from the fact that all the extra terms on the right hand side of the above are negative by definition. By rearranging terms we arrive at

\chi_{\boldsymbol{p}}(\alpha l)-\chi_{\boldsymbol{p}}(\sqrt{\alpha}l)\geq cl,

(51)

which obviously implies
l\leq\frac{\chi_{\boldsymbol{p}}({\alpha}l)}{c}

∎

Subsection 2
Proof of Theorem
3.1

The proof of Theorem
3.1
will be divided into two parts. First, we will derive the upper bound for the optimal transformation error
\epsilon_{N}
, Eq. (
10
). Then, we will provide a lower bound for
\epsilon_{N}
and show that it is approaching the derived upper bound in the asymptotic limit, and so we will prove Eq. (
9
).

Upper bound for the transformation error

We start by introducing the following averaged entropic quantities for the total initial distribution
\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}
:

\displaystyle\!\!h_{N}:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}H(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}H(\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}^{N}_{n})=:\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}h^{N}_{n},

(52a)

\displaystyle\!\!v_{N}:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}V(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}V(\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}^{N}_{n})=:\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}v^{N}_{n},

(52b)

\displaystyle\!\!y_{N}:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}Y(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}Y(\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}^{N}_{n})=:\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}y^{N}_{n}.

(52c)

Note that the above
v_{N}
and
y_{N}
are, up to rescaling by
N/\beta
, incoherent versions of
\sigma^{2}(F^{N})
and
\kappa^{3}(F^{N})
defined in Eqs. (
6b
)-(
6c
). We also define the function
l
:

\displaystyle l(z):=\exp\left(Nh_{N}+z\sqrt{Nv_{N}}\right).

(53)

We now rewrite the upper summation limit appearing in Eq. (
45
) employing the above function:

\exp[H(\hat{\boldsymbol{G}}^{N})+H(\tilde{\boldsymbol{f}}^{N}_{k})-H(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{N})]=l(x),

(54)

so that

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=\frac{D(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{G}}^{N})-D(\tilde{\boldsymbol{f}}^{N}_{k}\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{N})}{\sqrt{V(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})}}.

(55)

This can be further transformed by employing the invariance of relative entropic quantities under embedding, Eqs. (
38a
)-(
38b
), leading to

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum\limits_{n=1}^{N}D(\boldsymbol{p}_{n}^{N}\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{n}^{N})-D(\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{k}^{N}\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{N})}{\left(\sum\limits_{n=1}^{N}V(\boldsymbol{p}_{n}^{N}\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{n}^{N})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}},

(56)

which is precisely the argument of
\Phi
appearing in the statement of Theorem
3.1
in Eq. (
9
):
x=\Delta F^{N}/\sigma(F^{N})
. We conclude that with this
x
, we can then rewrite the expression for the optimal transformation error, Eq. (
45
), as

\epsilon_{N}=1-\sum_{i=1}^{l(x)}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}^{\downarrow}.

(57)

Next, we will find an upper bound for the error employing Eq. (
47a
):

\displaystyle\epsilon_{N}

\displaystyle\leq 1-\sum_{i=1}^{\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}(l(x))}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}^{\downarrow}=1-\sum_{i}\left\{\hat{P}^{N}_{i}\middle|\hat{P}^{N}_{i}\geq\frac{1}{l(x)}\right\}\!.

(58)

In order to evaluate the above sum, consider
N
discrete random variables
X_{n}
taking values
-\log(\hat{\v{p}}^N_n)_{i}
with probability
(\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}^{N}_{n})_{i}
, so that

\displaystyle\langle X_{n}\rangle

\displaystyle=h^{N}_{n},

(59a)

\displaystyle\langle(X_{n}-\langle X_{n}\rangle)^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=v^{N}_{n},

(59b)

\displaystyle\langle\left|X_{n}-\langle X_{n}\rangle\right|^{3}\rangle

\displaystyle=y^{N}_{n},

(59c)

where the average
\langle\cdot\rangle
is taken with respect to the distribution
\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}^{N}
. We then have the following

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\left\{\hat{P}^{N}_{i}\middle|\hat{P}^{N}_{i}\geq\frac{1}{l(x)}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{N}}\left\{\prod_{n=1}^{N}(\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}^{N}_{n})_{i_{n}}\middle|\prod_{n=1}^{N}(\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}^{N}_{n})_{i_{n}}\geq\frac{1}{l(x)}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{N}}\left\{\prod_{n=1}^{N}(\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}^{N}_{n})_{i_{n}}\middle|-\sum_{n=1}^{N}\log(\hat{\v{p}}^N_{n})_{i_{n}}\leq\log l(x)\right\}

\displaystyle=\Pr\left[\sum_{n=1}^{N}X_{n}\leq Nh_{N}+x\sqrt{Nv_{N}}\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr\left[\frac{\sum_{n=1}^{N}(X_{n}-\langle X_{n}\rangle)}{\sqrt{\sum_{n=1}^{N}\langle(X_{n}-\langle X_{n}\rangle)^{2}\rangle}}\leq x\right].

(60)

Now, the Berry-Esseen theorem
[
230
,
231
]
tells us that

\displaystyle\left|\Pr\!\left[\!\frac{\sum_{n=1}^{N}(X_{n}-\langle X_{n}\rangle)}{\sqrt{\sum_{n=1}^{N}\langle(X_{n}\!-\!\langle X_{n}\rangle)^{2}\rangle}}\leq x\!\right]\!-\!\Phi(x)\right|\!\leq\!\frac{Cy_{N}}{\sqrt{Nv_{N}^{3}}},\!\!

(61)

where
C
is a constant that was bounded in Refs.
[
232
,
233
]
by

0.4097\leq C\leq 0.4748.

(62)

We thus have

\left|\sum_{i}\left\{\hat{P}^{N}_{i}\middle|\hat{P}^{N}_{i}\geq\frac{1}{l(x)}\right\}-\Phi(x)\right|\leq\frac{Cy_{N}}{\sqrt{Nv_{N}^{3}}},

(63)

and so we conclude that the error
\epsilon_{N}
is bounded from above by

\epsilon_{N}\leq\Phi\left(-\frac{\Delta F^{N}}{\sigma(F^{N})}\right)+\frac{C\kappa^{3}(F^{N})}{\sigma^{3}(F^{N})},

(64)

which proves the single-shot upper bound on transformation error, Eq. (
10
), presented in Theorem
3.1
. Also, note that from Eq. (
64
), it is clear that if
\lim_{N\to\infty}v_{n}
and
\lim_{N\to\infty}y_{n}
are well-defined and non-zero (as we assume), then

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\epsilon_{N}\leq\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\Phi\left(-\frac{\Delta F^{N}}{\sigma(F^{N})}\right).

(65)

Lower bound for the transformation error

In order to lower bound the expression for the optimal error in the asymptotic limit, we choose
\alpha=\exp(\delta\sqrt{N})
with
\delta>0
in Eq. (
47b
). Thus, from Eq. (
49
) and Eq. (
53
) we have

l(x)\leq\frac{\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}[e^{\delta\sqrt{N}}l(x)]}{c}=\frac{\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}[l(x+\delta)]}{c},

(66)

where we can evaluate
c
from Eq. (
48
):

\displaystyle c=e^{\frac{\delta\sqrt{N}}{2}}\sum_{i=\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}[l(x+\delta/2)]}^{\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}[l(x+\delta)]}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}^{\downarrow}=e^{\frac{\delta\sqrt{N}}{2}}\Biggl(\sum_{i}

\displaystyle\biggl\{\hat{P}^{N}_{i}\bigg|\hat{P}^{N}_{i}\geq\frac{1}{l(x+\delta)}\biggl\}

\displaystyle-\sum_{i}\hat{P}^{N}_{i}\bigg|\hat{P}^{N}_{i}\geq\frac{1}{l(x+\delta/2)}\Biggl).

(67)

Using Eq. (
63
) we can bound the above expression from below as

c\geq e^{\frac{\delta\sqrt{N}}{2}}\Bigg[\Phi(x+\delta)-\Phi(x+\delta/2)-\frac{2Cy_{N}}{\sqrt{Nv_{N}^{3}}}\Bigg].

(68)

Now, for any finite
\delta>0
it is clear that there exists
N_{0}
such that for all
N\geq N_{0}
we have
c>1
. Combining with Eq. (
66
), for large enough
N
we finally have

l(x)\leq\frac{\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}[l(x+\delta)]}{c}\leq\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}[l(x+\delta)].

(69)

Hence, using Eq. (
69
), we have the following lower bound on transformation error

\displaystyle\epsilon_{N}=1-\sum_{i=1}^{l(x)}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}^{\downarrow}

\displaystyle\geq 1-\sum_{i=1}^{\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}[l(x+\delta)]}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}^{\downarrow}

\displaystyle=1-\sum_{i}\left\{\hat{P}^{N}_{i}\middle|\hat{P}^{N}_{i}\geq\frac{1}{l(x+\delta)}\right\}

\displaystyle\geq 1-\Phi(x+\delta)-\frac{Cy_{N}}{\sqrt{Nv_{N}^{3}}},

(70)

where in the last line we used Eq. (
63
) again. It is thus clear that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\epsilon_{N}\geq 1-\lim_{N\to\infty}\Phi(x+\delta)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\Phi(-x-\delta)

(71)

and, since it works for any
\delta>0
, we conclude that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\epsilon_{N}\geq\lim_{N\to\infty}\Phi\left[-\frac{\Delta F^{N}}{\sigma(F^{N})}\right].

(72)

Combining the above with the bound obtained in Eq. (
65
), we arrive at

\lim_{N\to\infty}\epsilon_{N}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\Phi\left[-\frac{\Delta F^{N}}{\sigma(F^{N})}\right],

(73)

which proves the asymptotic expression for the transformation error, Eq. (
9
), presented in Theorem
3.1
.

Subsection 3
Proof of Theorem
3.2

The proof of Theorem
3.2
will be divided into two parts. First, we will find the embedded version of the optimal final state minimising the dissipation of free energy
F^{N}_{\mathrm{diss}}
, and derive the expression for
F^{N}_{\mathrm{diss}}
as a function of the initial state. Then, we will calculate
F^{N}_{\mathrm{diss}}
up to second order asymptotic terms by upper and lower bounding the expression, and showing that the bounds coincide.

Deriving optimal dissipation

We start by presenting an extension of Lemma
5.2
that not only specifies the optimal transformation error for approximate majorisation but also yields the optimal final state.

Lemma 5.4

(Optimal final state)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{p}
and
\boldsymbol{q}
be distributions of size
d
with
V(\boldsymbol{q})=0
and
H(\boldsymbol{q})=\log L
. Then, states
\boldsymbol{r}
saturating
\boldsymbol{p}\succ_{\epsilon}\boldsymbol{q}
, i.e., such that
\boldsymbol{p}\succ\boldsymbol{r}
and
F(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{r})=1-\epsilon
with
\epsilon
being the minimal value specified by Lemma
5.2
, are given by
\Pi\boldsymbol{q}^{*}
, where
\Pi
is an arbitrary permutation,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}^{*}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\frac{1-\epsilon}{L}&\quad\mathrm{for\penalty\ }i\leq L,\\
B\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}_{i-L}&\quad\mathrm{for\penalty\ }i>L,\\
\end{cases}\,

(74)

B
is an arbitrary
(d-L)\times(d-L)
bistochastic matrix, and
\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}
is a vector of size
(d-L)
with
p^{\prime}_{i}=p^{\downarrow}_{i+L}
. Moreover, among all such distributions
\Pi\boldsymbol{q}^{*}
, the entropy is minimised for the one with
B
being the identity matrix.

The proof of the above lemma can be found in Section
7
. Here, we apply it to the central Eq. (
36
) that specifies the conditions for the investigated
\epsilon
-approximate thermodynamic distillation process. As a result, the actual total final state in the embedded picture,
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}^{N}
, which is
\epsilon
away from the target state
\hat{\boldsymbol{G}}^{N}\otimes\tilde{\boldsymbol{f}}^{N}_{k}
, is given, up to permutations, by

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}^{N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\frac{1-\epsilon}{K}&\quad\mathrm{for\penalty\ }i\leq K,\\
(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}\otimes\tilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{N})_{i}^{\downarrow}&\quad\mathrm{for\penalty\ }i>K.\\
\end{cases}\,

(75)

where

K=\exp(H(\hat{\v{G}}^N)+H(\tilde{\v{f}}^N_k)).

(76)

Note that we have chosen
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}^{N}
to minimise entropy since, due to Eq. (
38a
), it translates into the real (unembedded) final state
\boldsymbol{F}^{N}
with maximal free energy, i.e., it leads to minimal free energy dissipation.

The next step consists of going from the embedded to the unembedded picture. Importantly, if a state in the embedded picture is not uniform within each embedding box, the unembedding will effectively lead to the loss of free energy. However, we note that the final state is given by Eq. (
75
) only up to permutations. Thus, one may freely rearrange its elements to minimise such a loss. In particular, in Section
10
we show that for the case of identical initial states (i.e.,
\boldsymbol{P}^{n}=\boldsymbol{p}^{\otimes N}
), there exists a permutation that transforms
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}^{N}
so that it is uniform in almost all embedding boxes, which leads to exponentially small dissipation of free energy (i.e., no dissipation up to second-order asymptotics). Therefore, employing the definition of dissipated free energy from Eq. (
8
) and the above discussion, we have

\displaystyle F^{N}_{\mathrm{diss}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}\left(D(\boldsymbol{P}^{N}\|\boldsymbol{G}^{N})-D(\boldsymbol{F}^{N}\|\boldsymbol{G}^{N}\otimes\tilde{\boldsymbol{G}}^{N})\right)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}\left(H(\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}^{N})-H(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})-\log\tilde{D}\right).

(77)

Here,
\tilde{D}
is the embedding constant defined by
\tilde{\boldsymbol{G}}^{N}
according to Eq. (
20
), and similarly
D
will denote this embedding constant for
\boldsymbol{G}^{N}
.
Note, however, that these can always be chosen to be equal, since the only thing that matters is that
\tilde{G}^{N}_{k}=\tilde{D}_{k}/\tilde{D}
and
{{G}}^{N}_{k}={D}_{k}/D
, i.e., the change in
\tilde{D}
or
D
can be compensated by the appropriate change in
\tilde{D}_{k}
or
D_{k}
.

Next, noting that
K=D\tilde{D}_{k}=\tilde{D}\tilde{D}_{k}
, we calculate the entropy of
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}^{N}
:

\displaystyle H(\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}^{N})=

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{K}\frac{1-\epsilon}{K}\log\left(\frac{1-\epsilon}{K}\right)-\sum_{i>\tilde{D}_{k}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}{{}^{\downarrow}}\log\frac{(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}{{}^{\downarrow}}}{\tilde{D}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(1-\epsilon)\log(1-\epsilon)+(1-\epsilon)\log K

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-\sum_{i>\tilde{D}_{k}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}{{}^{\downarrow}}\log(\hat{\v{P}}^N)_{i}{{}^{\downarrow}}+\epsilon\log\tilde{D}

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\log\tilde{D}+(1-\epsilon)\log\tilde{D}_{k}-\sum_{i>\tilde{D}_{k}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}{{}^{\downarrow}}\log(\hat{\v{P}}^N)_{i}{{}^{\downarrow}},

(78)

where we have dropped the term
(1-\epsilon)\log(1-\epsilon)
as it is constant (i.e. it does not scale with
N
).
Therefore, the dissipated free energy in the optimal distillation process is simply given by

\!\!F^{N}_{\text{diss}}\simeq\frac{1}{\beta}\!\left(\!(1-\epsilon)\log\tilde{D}_{k}+\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{D}_{k}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}{{}^{\downarrow}}\log(\hat{\v{P}}^N)_{i}{{}^{\downarrow}}\!\right)\!.\!

(79)

Calculating optimal dissipation

We now proceed to provide bounds for
F^{N}_{\text{diss}}
. To do so, we first make use of Eq.(
49
) and the fact that
\log x
is negative for all
x\in(0,1)
to write

\displaystyle\!\!\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{D}_{k}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}{{}^{\downarrow}}\log(\hat{\v{P}}^N)_{i}{{}^{\downarrow}}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}(\tilde{D}_{k})}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}{{}^{\downarrow}}\log(\hat{\v{P}}^N)_{i}{{}^{\downarrow}}\!.\!

(80)

The right hand side of the above can then be recast as follows,

\displaystyle\!\!\sum_{i=1}^{\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}(\tilde{D}_{k})}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}{{}^{\downarrow}}\log(\hat{\v{P}}^N)_{i}{{}^{\downarrow}}=\sum_{i}\left\{\hat{P}^{N}_{i}\log(\hat{P}^N_i)\middle|\hat{P}^{N}_{i}\geq\frac{1}{\tilde{D}_{k}}\right\}

\displaystyle\penalty\ =\!\!\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{N}}\!\left\{\prod_{n=1}^{N}(\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}^{N}_{n})_{i_{n}}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\log(\hat{\v{p}}^N_{m})_{i_{m}}\middle|\prod_{n=1}^{N}(\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}^{N}_{n})_{i_{n}}\geq\frac{1}{\tilde{D}_{k}}\!\right\}

\displaystyle\penalty\ =\!\!\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{N}}\!\left\{\prod_{n=1}^{N}(\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}^{N}_{n})_{i_{n}}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\log(\hat{\v{p}}^N_{m})_{i_{m}}\right.\times

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\times\left|-\sum_{m=1}^{N}\log(\hat{\v{p}}^N_{m})_{i_{m}}\leq\log\tilde{D}_{k}\right\}.

(81)

Let us now note that from the definition in Eq. (
7
) we have (here we employ the notation introduced in Eqs. (
52a
)-(
52c
)):

\displaystyle\beta\Delta F^{N}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n=1}^{N}D(\boldsymbol{p}_{n}^{N}\|\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{n}^{N})-D(\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{k}^{N}\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{N})

\displaystyle=H(\tilde{\boldsymbol{f}}_{k}^{N})-\sum\limits_{n=1}^{N}H(\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}_{n}^{N})=\log\tilde{D}_{k}-Nh_{N}.

(82)

Next, since for non-trivial error we need

\beta\Delta F^{N}=x\beta\sigma(F^{N})=x\sqrt{Nv_{N}},

(83)

with some constant
x
, we can rewrite
\log\tilde{D}_{k}
as

\log\tilde{D}_{k}=Nh_{N}+x\sqrt{Nv_{N}}.

(84)

Coming back to Eq. (
81
), we can rewrite it by introducing
N
discrete random variables
\{X_{n}\}_{n=1}^{N}
assuming values
-\log(\hat{\v{p}}_n^N)_{i_{n}}
with probability
(\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}^{N}_{n})_{i_{n}}
. Crucially, since the sum of their averages is
Nh_{N}
and their total variance is
Nv_{N}
, the condition in the summation in Eq. (
81
) simply becomes:

Y_{N}:=\frac{\sum_{n=1}^{N}(X_{n}-\langle X_{n}\rangle)}{\sqrt{\sum_{n=1}^{N}\langle(X_{n}-\langle X_{n}\rangle)^{2}\rangle}}\leq x.

(85)

Thus, we can write Eq. (
81
) in a compact way as follows

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}(\tilde{D}_{k})}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}{{}^{\downarrow}}\log(\hat{\v{P}}^N)_{i}{{}^{\downarrow}}=-\int_{T}\sum_{n=1}^{N}X_{N}dP,

(86)

where
P
is discrete probability measure given by
P(i_{1},\ldots,i_{N})=\prod_{n=1}^{N}(\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}^{N}_{n})_{i_{n}}
and
T
is a region satisfying the constraint
Y_{N}<x
. Noting that

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N}X_{n}=\sqrt{Nv_{n}}Y_{N}+Nh_{N}

(87)

we can further rewrite it as

\displaystyle\!\!\!\int\limits_{T}\sum_{n=1}^{N}X_{N}dP=\sqrt{Nv_{N}}\!\!\int\limits_{Y_{N}\leq x}\!\!Y_{N}dP+Nh_{N}\!\!\int\limits_{Y_{N}\leq x}\!\!dP.\!

(88)

The second integral on the right hand side of the above was already calculated and is equal to
1-\epsilon
, where
\epsilon
is the optimal transformation error from Theorem
3.1
. Further, since
Y_{N}
is a standarized sum of independent random variables, its distribution tends to a normal Gaussian distribution with density denoted by
\phi(x)
, i.e.,

\int\limits_{Y_{N}\leq x}\!\!Y_{N}dP\simeq\int_{-\infty}^{x}y\phi(y)dy=-\frac{e^{-x^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}.

(89)

We thus obtain:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}(\tilde{D}_{k})}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}{{}^{\downarrow}}\log(\hat{\v{P}}^N)_{i}{{}^{\downarrow}}

\displaystyle\simeq\sqrt{Nv_{N}}\frac{e^{-x^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}-Nh_{N}(1-\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{Nv_{N}}\frac{e^{-x^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}-(1-\epsilon)(\log\tilde{D}_{k}-x\sqrt{Nv_{N}})

\displaystyle=\beta\sigma(F^{N})\!\left[\!\frac{e^{-x^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}+x(1-\epsilon)\!\right]\!-\!(1-\epsilon)\log\tilde{D}_{k}.

(90)

We can now use the inequality from Eq. (
80
) and substitute the above to Eq. (
79
) to arrive at:

F^{N}_{\text{diss}}\lesssim\sigma(F^{N})\left(\frac{e^{-x^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}+x(1-\epsilon)\right).

(91)

Next, employing Eq. (
83
) and the expression for optimal transformation error from Theorem
3.1
, we can re-express
x
as

x=-\Phi^{-1}(\epsilon)

(92)

to finally obtain

F^{N}_{\text{diss}}\lesssim\sigma(F^{N})\left[\frac{e^{\frac{-(\Phi^{-1}(\epsilon))^{2}}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}-\Phi^{-1}(\epsilon)(1-\epsilon)\right].

(93)

To provide a lower bound of
F^{N}_{\text{diss}}
given in Eq. (
79
), we simply follow the argument we have given in Sec.
2
. From Eq. (
47b
) it straightforwardly follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{D}_{k}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}^{\downarrow}\log(\hat{\v{P}}^N)_{i}^{\downarrow}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}(\tilde{D}_{k}e^{\delta\sqrt{N}})}{c}}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}^{\downarrow}\log(\hat{\v{P}}^N)_{i}^{\downarrow},

(94)

where
c
, as before, can be lower bounded by 1 for large enough
N
. We thus get

F^{N}_{\mathrm{diss}}\gtrsim\frac{1}{\beta}\left[(1-\epsilon)\log\tilde{D}_{k}+\sum_{i=1}^{\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}(\tilde{D}_{k}e^{\delta\sqrt{N}})}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}^{\downarrow}\log(\hat{\v{P}}^N)_{i}^{\downarrow}\right].

(95)

Since the above inequality holds for any
\delta>0
, therefore the limit
\delta\rightarrow 0
we have

F^{N}_{\mathrm{diss}}\gtrsim\sigma(F^{N})\left\{\frac{e^{\frac{-[\Phi^{-1}(\epsilon)]^{2}}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}-\Phi^{-1}(\epsilon)(1-\epsilon)\right\}.

(96)

Combining the above with the upper bound from Eq. (
93
) we finally arrive at

F^{N}_{\text{diss}}\simeq a(\epsilon)\sigma(F^{N}),

(97)

where
a(\epsilon)
is given by Eq. (
13
).

Subsection 4
Proof of Theorem
3.3

The proof of Theorem
3.3
will be divided into three parts. First, we will show that a thermodynamic distillation process from a general state
\rho
can be reduced to a distillation process from an incoherent state that is a dephased version of
\rho
. Employing this observation, we will recast the problem under consideration in terms of approximate majorisation and thermomajorisation as described in Sec.
1
. Then, in the second part of the proof, we will derive the upper bound for the optimal transformation error
\epsilon_{N}
. Finally, in the third part, we will provide a lower bound for
\epsilon_{N}
and show that it is approaching the derived upper bound in the asymptotic limit, and so we will prove Eq. (
16
).

Reducing the problem to the incoherent case

The thermodynamic distillation problem under investigation is specified as follows. The family of initial systems consists of a collection of
N
identical subsystems, each with the same Hamiltonian

H=\sum_{i=1}^{d}E_{i}\outerproduct{E_i}{E_i},

(98)

and an ancillary system with an arbitrary Hamiltonian
H_{A}
(note that the ancillary system can always be ignored by simply choosing its dimension to be 1). The family of initial states is given by

\rho^{N}=\psi^{\otimes N}\otimes\outerproduct{E^A_0}{E^A_0},

(99)

where

\psi=\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi},\quad\ket{\psi}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sqrt{p_{i}}e^{i\phi_{i}}\ket{E_i},

(100)

is an arbitrary pure state and
\ket{E^A_0}
is an eigenstate of
H_{A}
with energy
E^{A}_{0}
. The family of target systems is composed of subsystems described by arbitrary Hamiltonians
\tilde{H}^{N}
and a subsystem described by the Hamiltonian
H_{A}
. The family of target states is given by

\tilde{\rho}^{N}=\outerproduct{\tilde{E}^{N}_k}{\tilde{E}^{N}_k}\otimes\outerproduct{E^A_1}{E^A_1},

(101)

where
\ket{\tilde{E}^{N}_k}
is some eigenstate of
\tilde{H}^{N}
and
\ket{E^A_1}
is an eigenstate of
H_{A}
with energy
E^{A}_{1}
. We are thus interested in the existence of a thermal operation
\mathcal{E}^{\beta}
approximately performing the following transformation:

\psi^{\otimes N}\otimes\outerproduct{E^A_0}{E^A_0}\penalty\ \xrightarrow{\penalty\ \mathcal{E}^{\beta}\penalty\ }\penalty\ \outerproduct{\tilde{E}^{N}_k}{\tilde{E}^{N}_k}\otimes\outerproduct{E^A_1}{E^A_1}.

(102)

We now have the following simple, but very useful, lemma.

Lemma 5.5

(Dephasing invariance of TOs)
.

Every incoherent state
\sigma
achievable from a state
\rho
through a thermal operation is also achievable from
\mathcal{D}(\rho)
, where
\mathcal{D}
is the dephasing operation destroying coherence between different energy subspaces:

\displaystyle\exists\mathcal{E}^{\beta}:\mathcal{E}^{\beta}(\rho)=\sigma\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathcal{E}^{\beta}[\mathcal{D}(\rho)]=\sigma.

(103)

Proof.

First, for a given
\rho
and incoherent
\sigma
, assume that there exists a thermal operation
\mathcal{E}^{\beta}
such that
\mathcal{E}^{\beta}(\rho)=\sigma
. Now, employing the fact that every thermal operation is covariant with respect to time-translations
[
234
]
, and using the fact that incoherent
\sigma
by definition satisfies
\mathcal{D}(\sigma)=\sigma
, we get

\mathcal{E}^{\beta}[\mathcal{D}(\rho)]=\mathcal{D}[\mathcal{E}^{\beta}(\rho)]=\mathcal{D}(\sigma)=\sigma.

(104)

Likewise, the reverse implication holds by noting that the dephasing operation is a thermal operation.
∎

Because the target state in our case is incoherent, we can use the above result to restate our problem as the existence of a thermal operation
\mathcal{E}^{\beta}
approximately performing the following transformation

\mathcal{D}(\psi^{\otimes N}\otimes\outerproduct{E^A_0}{E^A_0})\penalty\ \xrightarrow{\penalty\ \mathcal{E}^{\beta}\penalty\ }\penalty\ \outerproduct{\tilde{E}^{N}_k}{\tilde{E}^{N}_k}\otimes\outerproduct{E^A_1}{E^A_1}.

(105)

Since

\mathcal{D}(\psi^{\otimes N}\otimes\outerproduct{E^A_0}{E^A_0})=\mathcal{D}(\psi^{\otimes N})\otimes\outerproduct{E^A_0}{E^A_0},

(106)

our problem further reduces to understanding the structure of the incoherent state
\mathcal{D}(\psi^{\otimes N})
. It is block-diagonal in the energy eigenbasis and can be diagonalised using thermal operations (since unitaries in a fixed energy subspace are free operations). After such a procedure, we end up with an incoherent state that is described by the probability distribution
\boldsymbol{P}^{N}
over the multi-index set
\boldsymbol{k}

P^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\binom{N}{k_{1},...,k_{d}}\prod_{i=1}^{d}p^{k_{i}}_{i}.

(107)

Note that
P^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
specifies the probability of
k_{1}
systems being in energy state
E_{1}
,
k_{2}
systems being in energy state
E_{2}
, and so on; and that we made a technical assumption that energy levels are incommensurable, so that each vector
\boldsymbol{k}
corresponds to a different value of total energy.

We have thus reduced the problem of thermodynamic distillation from pure states to thermodynamic distillation from incoherent states. More precisely, let us denote the sharp distributions corresponding to
\ket{E^A_i}
by
\boldsymbol{s}^{A}_{i}
and the corresponding flat states after embedding by
\boldsymbol{f}^{A}_{i}
. As before, we also use
\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{k}^{N}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{f}}_{k}^{N}
to denote distributions related to the sharp state
\ket{\tilde{E}^{N}_k}
and its corresponding flat state. The embedded Gibbs state corresponding to
H^{N}
will be again denoted by
\hat{\boldsymbol{G}}^{N}
, however now it has an even simpler form than in Eq. (
35
), as the initial systems have identical Hamiltonians:

\hat{\boldsymbol{G}}^{N}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}^{\otimes N}={\boldsymbol{\eta}}^{\otimes N}.

(108)

Similarly,
\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}
will be used to denote the embedded initial state (even though it now has a different form than in Eq. (
35
)):

\hat{P}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k},g_{\boldsymbol{k}}}=\binom{N}{k_{1},...,k_{d}}\prod^{d}_{i=1}\frac{p^{k_{i}}_{i}}{D^{k_{i}}_{i}},

(109)

where
\gamma_{i}=D_{i}/D
and

g_{\boldsymbol{k}}\in\bigg\{1,...,\prod_{i=1}^{d}D^{k_{i}}_{i}\bigg\}

(110)

is an index for the degeneracy coming from embedding. With the notation set, our distillation problem can now be written as

{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}}^{N}\otimes\boldsymbol{f}^{A}_{0}\otimes\tilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}}\succ_{\epsilon}\hat{\boldsymbol{G}}^{N}\otimes\boldsymbol{f}^{A}_{1}\otimes\tilde{\boldsymbol{f}_{k}}.

(111)

Upper bound for the transformation error

We begin by observing that our target distribution in Eq. (
111
) is flat, and so
V(\hat{\boldsymbol{G}}^{N}\otimes\boldsymbol{f}^{A}_{1}\otimes\tilde{\boldsymbol{f}_{k}})=0
. Thus, we can employ Lemma
5.2
and Eq. (
43
) to get the following expression for the optimal transformation error:

\epsilon_{N}=1-\sum_{j=1}^{L}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})^{\downarrow}_{j}

(112)

where
L
is given by

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\exp[H(\hat{\boldsymbol{G}}^{N})+H(\boldsymbol{f}^{A}_{1})+H(\tilde{\boldsymbol{f}}_{k})-H(\boldsymbol{f}^{A}_{0})-H(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}})]

\displaystyle=\exp[H(\hat{\boldsymbol{G}}^{N})-D(\tilde{\boldsymbol{f}}_{k}\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}})-\beta(E_{1}^{A}-E_{0}^{A})].

(113)

Notice that in the current case
\Delta F^{N}
, defined in Eq. (
7
), is given by

\displaystyle\!\!\!\Delta F^{N}

\displaystyle\!=\!\frac{1}{\beta}\Big(D(\psi^{\otimes N}\|\gamma^{\otimes N})+D(\outerproduct{E_0^A}{E_0^A}\|\gamma_{A})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-D(\outerproduct{\tilde{E}^N_{k}}{\tilde{E}^N_{k}}\|\tilde{{\gamma}})-D(\outerproduct{E_1^A}{E_1^A}\|\gamma_{A})\Big)

\displaystyle\!=\!\frac{1}{\beta}\Big(ND(\psi\|\gamma)\!-\!D(\tilde{\boldsymbol{f}}_{k}\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}})\!-\!\beta(E_{1}^{A}\!-\!E_{0}^{A})\Big).

(114)

Using the above we can then rewrite
L
as

\displaystyle\log L=

\displaystyle\beta\Delta F^{N}+H(\hat{\boldsymbol{G}}^{N})-ND(\psi\|\gamma).

(115)

Now, employing Eq. (
47a
) and the above, we provide the upper bound for
\epsilon_{N}
:

\displaystyle\epsilon_{N}

\displaystyle\leq 1-\sum_{\boldsymbol{k},g_{\boldsymbol{k}}}\left\{\hat{P}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k},g_{\boldsymbol{k}}}\middle|\hat{P}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k},g_{\boldsymbol{k}}}\geq\frac{1}{L}\right\}=1-\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\left\{{P}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\middle|{P}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\geq\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{d}D_{i}^{k_{i}}}{L}\right\}

\displaystyle=1-\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\left\{{P}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\middle|\log{P}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{d}k_{i}\log D_{i}-\log L\right\}

\displaystyle=1-\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\bigg\{{P}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\Bigg|\frac{\log{P}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}}}{N}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{k_{i}}{N}\log\gamma_{i}+D(\psi\|\gamma)-\frac{\beta}{N}\Delta F^{N}\Bigg\}.

(116)

To simplify the calculation of the upper bound of
\epsilon_{N}
, we rewrite each
\boldsymbol{k}
as a function of a vector
\boldsymbol{s}
such that

\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s})=N\boldsymbol{p}+\sqrt{N}\boldsymbol{s},

(117)

with
\sum_{i=1}^{d}s_{i}=0
. We then note that

D(\psi\|\gamma)=-\sum_{i=1}^{d}p_{i}\log\gamma_{i}

(118)

and so the condition in Eq. (
116
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\frac{\log{P}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s})}}{N}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{i=1}^{d}s_{i}\log\gamma_{i}-\frac{\beta}{N}\Delta F^{N}=-\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{i=1}^{d}s_{i}E_{i}-\frac{\beta}{N}\Delta F^{N}.

(119)

As we rigorously argue in Section
8
, the left-hand side of the above vanishes much quicker than the right-hand side when
N\to\infty
, leading to

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\epsilon_{N}\leq 1\!-\!\lim_{N\to\infty}\sum_{\boldsymbol{s}}\bigg\{{P}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s})}\Bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{d}s_{i}E_{i}\geq-\frac{\Delta F^{N}}{\sqrt{N}}\Bigg\}.

(120)

Figure 3:

Standardising the bivariate normal distribution
. The points with equal probability density for the bivariate normal distribution are represented by a red ellipsis centred at the origin, and the black dashed line corresponds to the constraining hyperplane. The upper bound on
\epsilon_{N}
is given by the probability mass within the shaded area. In order to calculate it, we first apply a rotation and scaling transformation, making the ellipsis symmetric with respect to the origin. Then, using the rotational symmetry of the standard bivariate normal distribution, one can rotate it such that the hyperplane becomes parallel to
x_{2}
.

Our goal is then to calculate the sum of
P^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s})}
in the limit
N\to\infty
subject to the following hyperplane constraint

\boldsymbol{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{E}\geq-\frac{\Delta F^{N}}{\sqrt{N}},

(121)

where
\boldsymbol{E}
is a vector of energies (eigenvalues of
H
). First, we approximate the multinomial distribution
\boldsymbol{P}^{N}
specified in Eq. (
107
) by a multivariate normal distribution
\mathcal{N}^{(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}
with mean vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}=N\boldsymbol{p}
and covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=N(\text{diag }(\boldsymbol{p})-\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{T})
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{d}|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}({\boldsymbol{k}}-{\boldsymbol{\mu}})^{T}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{k}}-{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{d}|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{s}^{T}{N\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1}\boldsymbol{s}\right).

(122)

As we explain in Section
9
, such an approximation can always be made with an error approaching 0 as
N\rightarrow\infty
. Next, we standardise the multivariate normal distribution
\mathcal{N}^{(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}
using rotation and scaling transformations:

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\Theta^{T}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}\Theta,

(123)

where
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
is a diagonal matrix with the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
and
\Theta
is an orthogonal matrix with columns given by the eigenvectors of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
. We illustrate this process for a three-level system (so described by
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
since
\sum_{i}s_{i}=0
) in Fig.
3
. This rotation and scaling of co-ordinates allows us to write
\mathcal{N}^{(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}
as a product of univariate standard normal distribution
\phi(y_{i})
:

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\mathcal{N}^{(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{y}))}

\displaystyle\!=\!\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{d}|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|}}\exp\left(\!-\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{y}^{T}\boldsymbol{y}\!\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\phi(y_{i}),\!

(124)

where

\boldsymbol{y}=\sqrt{N}(\Theta^{T}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}})^{-1}\boldsymbol{s}.

(125)

We then can equivalently write the equation specifying the hyperplane, Eq. (
121
), as

(\Theta^{T}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}\boldsymbol{y})\cdot\boldsymbol{E}\geq-\Delta F^{N}.

(126)

Observe that the standard normal distribution given in Eq. (
124
) is rotationally invariant about the origin. One can thus choose a coordinate system
\boldsymbol{x}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}\}
by applying a suitable rotation
R
on
\boldsymbol{y}=\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{d}\}
, so that the hyperplane specified in Eq. (
121
) becomes parallel to all coordinate axes but the
x_{1}
axis. Eq. (
124
) can then be rewritten in the following form

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\mathcal{N}^{(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{x}))}

\displaystyle\!=\!\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{d}|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|}}\exp\left(\!-\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{x}^{T}\boldsymbol{x}\!\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\phi(x_{i}).\!

(127)

As we have

\boldsymbol{x}=R\boldsymbol{y},

(128)

we can use it together with Eq. (
125
) to rewrite Eq. (
121
) as

\Theta^{T}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}R^{T}\boldsymbol{x}\cdot\boldsymbol{E}\geq-\Delta F^{N}.

(129)

To calculate the right hand side of the inequality given in Eq. (
120
) in the limit
N\rightarrow\infty
, we integrate Eq.
(
127
)
from
-\infty
to
d_{\mathcal{O}}
along
x_{1}
, and from
-\infty
to
+\infty
along any other
x_{i}\neq x_{1}
, where
d_{\mathcal{O}}
is the signed distance of the hyperplane given in Eq. (
129
) from the origin (see Fig.
3
). This distance can be explicitly calculated as

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta F^{N}}{\sqrt{\boldsymbol{E}\cdot(R\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}\Theta)^{T}(R\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}\Theta)\boldsymbol{E}}}=\frac{\Delta F^{N}}{\sqrt{\boldsymbol{E}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{E})}}=\frac{\Delta F^{N}}{\sigma(F^{N})},

(130)

where we have used the definition of
\sigma(F^{N})
from Eq. (
6b
) in the last line. Thus, the upper bound on
\epsilon_{N}
in the limit
N\rightarrow\infty
given in Eq. (
120
) can be calculated as

\displaystyle 1\!-\!\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\!dx_{d}\phi(x_{d})\ldots\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\!dx_{2}\phi(x_{2})\int_{-\infty}^{d_{\mathcal{O}}}\!dx_{1}\phi(x_{1})

\displaystyle=1\!-\!\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\Phi(d_{\mathcal{O}})=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\Phi\left(-\frac{\Delta F^{N}}{\sigma(F^{N})}\right).

(131)

Lower bound for the transformation error

We start by writing
L
from Eq. (
115
) as

\displaystyle L=\exp\left(AN+x\sqrt{Nv_{N}}\right)=:L(x),

(132)

where

\displaystyle A=

\displaystyle H(\boldsymbol{\eta})-D(\psi\|{\gamma}),\qquad x=\frac{\Delta F^{N}}{\sigma(F^{N})}.

(133)

In the previous section we have exactly calculated the right hand side of Eq. (
116
) in the limit
N\rightarrow\infty
(see Eq. (
131
)). Using Eqs. (
132
)-(
133
), we can equivalently rewrite this as

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k},g_{\boldsymbol{k}}}\left\{\hat{P}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k},g_{\boldsymbol{k}}}\middle|\hat{P}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k},g_{\boldsymbol{k}}}\geq\frac{1}{L(x)}\right\}=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\Phi(x),

(134)

where
x
depends on
N
as per Eq. (
133
). Now, to prove the lower bound on transformation error
\epsilon_{N}
we start with the exact expression, Eq. (
112
), and use the inequality from Eq. (
47b
). Similarly as before, we choose
\alpha=\exp(\delta\sqrt{N})
such that
\delta>0
, in the Eq. (
47b
). Thus from Eq. (
49
) and Eq. (
132
) we have,

L(x)\leq\frac{\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}(e^{\delta\sqrt{N}}L(x))}{c}=\frac{\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}(L(x+\delta))}{c}

(135)

where
c
can be evaluated similarly as before:

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle=e^{\delta\sqrt{N}/2}\sum_{i=\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}(e^{\delta\sqrt{N}/2}L(x))}^{\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}(e^{\delta\sqrt{N}}L(x))}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}^{\downarrow}=e^{\delta\sqrt{N}/2}\sum_{i=\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}(L(x+\delta/2))}^{\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}(L(x+\delta))}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}^{\downarrow}

\displaystyle=e^{\delta\sqrt{N}/2}\left(\sum_{i}\left\{\hat{P}^{N}_{i}\middle|\hat{P}_{i}^{N}\geq\frac{1}{L(x+\delta)}\right\}\right.\left.-\sum_{i}\left\{\hat{P}^{N}_{i}\middle|\hat{P}_{i}^{N}\geq\frac{1}{L(x+\delta/2)}\right\}\right).

(136)

Using Eq. (
134
), we see that the limiting behaviour of
c
from Eq. (
136
) is given by

\lim_{N\to\infty}c=\left(\Phi(x+\delta)-\Phi(x+\delta/2)\right)\lim_{N\to\infty}e^{\delta\sqrt{N}/2}.

(137)

Thus, for any finite
\delta>0
, there always exists
N_{0}
such that for all
N\geq N_{0}
we have
c>1
. Combining with Eq. (
135
), for large enough
N
we finally have,

L(x)\leq\frac{\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}(L(x+\delta))}{c}\leq\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}(L(x+\delta)).

(138)

Hence, using Eq. (
138
), we have the following lower bound on transformation error

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\epsilon_{N}

\displaystyle\geq 1-\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}(L(x+\delta))}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}^{\downarrow}\,

\displaystyle=1-\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{i}\left\{\hat{P}^{N}_{i}\middle|\hat{P}_{i}^{N}\geq\frac{1}{L(x+\delta)}\right\}

\displaystyle=1-\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\Phi(x+\delta)=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\Phi(-x-\delta),

(139)

where the first equality in the last line follows from Eq. (
134
).
Since the above inequality holds for any
\delta>0
, taking the limit
\delta\rightarrow 0
we conclude that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\epsilon_{N}\geq\lim_{N\to\infty}\Phi\left(-\frac{\Delta F^{N}}{\sigma(F^{N})}\right).

(140)

Finally, combining the above with the bound obtained in Eq. (
131
), we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}\epsilon_{N}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\Phi\left(-\frac{\Delta F^{N}}{\sigma(F^{N})}\right),

(141)

which completes the proof.

Subsection 5
Proof of Theorem
3.4

The proof of Theorem
3.4
will be divided into two parts. First, we will find the embedded version of the optimal final state minimising the dissipation of free energy
F^{N}_{\mathrm{diss}}
, and derive the lower bound for
F^{N}_{\mathrm{diss}}
as a function of the initial state. And then, we will calculate this bound up to second order asymptotic terms.

Deriving bound for optimal dissipation

We start by applying Lemma
5.4
to the central Eq. (
111
) that specifies the conditions for the investigated
\epsilon
-approximate thermodynamic distillation process. As a result, the actual total final state in the embedded picture,
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}^{N}
, which is
\epsilon
away from the target state
\hat{\boldsymbol{G}}^{N}\otimes\boldsymbol{f}_{1}^{A}\otimes\tilde{\boldsymbol{f}}_{k}
, is given, up to permutations, by

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}^{N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\frac{1-\epsilon}{k}&\quad\mathrm{for\penalty\ }i\leq k,\\
({\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}}^{N}\otimes\boldsymbol{f}^{A}_{0}\otimes\tilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}})_{i}^{\downarrow}&\quad\mathrm{for\penalty\ }i>k,\end{cases}\,

(142)

where

k=\exp\Big(H(\hat{\v{G}}^N)+H(\v{f}^A_1)+H(\tilde{\v{f}}_k)\Big).

(143)

Similarly as before, we have chosen
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}^{N}
with minimal entropy (i.e., the bistochastic map
B
from Lemma
5.4
was chosen to be a permutation), as this minimises the dissipation of free energy. Due to the fact that the final state is specified only up to permutations, one may freely rearrange its elements to reduce the dissipation of free energy coming from unembedding (such dissipation happens when a state is not uniform within each embedding box). However, unlike in the previous case, this permutational freedom does not necessarily allow one to completely avoid additional dissipation. In the previous case, the number of non-uniform embedding boxes was exponentially small, which led to a negligible loss of free energy due to unembedding. But here, the number of non-uniform embedding boxes is not exponentially small and that can lead to a finite dissipation of free energy. Therefore, in this case, we can only provide a lower bound on dissipation of free energy as follows:

\displaystyle F^{N}_{\text{diss}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\beta}\Big[D(\psi^{\otimes N}\|\boldsymbol{G}^{N})+D(\outerproduct{E_0^A}{E_0^A}\|\gamma_{A})-D(\boldsymbol{F}^{N}\|\boldsymbol{G}^{N}\otimes\gamma_{A}\otimes\tilde{\boldsymbol{G}}^{N})\Big]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\beta}\Big[D(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{G}}^{N})+H(\boldsymbol{P}^{N})+D(\outerproduct{E_0^A}{E_0^A}\|\gamma_{A})

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt-D(\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}^{N}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{G}}^{N}\otimes\hat{\gamma}_{A}\otimes\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{G}}}^{N})\Big],

(144)

where we have used the fact that

\displaystyle D(\psi^{\otimes N}\|\boldsymbol{G}^{N})

\displaystyle=D(\mathcal{D}(\psi^{\otimes N})\|\boldsymbol{G}^{N})+D(\psi^{\otimes N}\|\mathcal{D}(\psi^{\otimes N}))

\displaystyle=D(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{G}}^{N})+H(\boldsymbol{P}^{N}).

(145)

Next, we recall that
H(\boldsymbol{P}^{N})=O(\log N)
(see Section
8
for details), and note that
D(\outerproduct{E_0^A}{E_0^A}\|\gamma_{A})=-\log\gamma_{0}^{A}
. Thus, the inequality from Eq. (
144
) can be simplified as follows:

\displaystyle F^{N}_{\text{diss}}\gtrsim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}\Big(D(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{G}}^{N})-D(\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}^{N}\|\hat{\boldsymbol{G}}^{N}\otimes\hat{\gamma}_{A}\otimes\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{G}}}^{N})-\log\gamma_{0}^{A}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}\Big(H(\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}^{N})-H(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})\!-\!\log(\tilde{D}D^A)-\log\gamma_{0}^{A}\Big).

(146)

Here,
\tilde{D}
and
D^{A}
are the embedding constants defined by Eq. (
20
) for
\tilde{\boldsymbol{G}}^{N}
and
\gamma_{A}
, respectively, and similarly
D
will denote this embedding constant for
\boldsymbol{G}^{N}
. As before,
\tilde{D}
and
D
can be chosen to be equal, since the only thing that matters is that
\tilde{{G}}^{N}_{k}=\tilde{D}_{k}/\tilde{D}
and
{{G}}^{N}_{k}={D}_{k}/D
, i.e., the change in
\tilde{D}
or
D
can be compensated by the appropriate change in
\tilde{D}_{k}
or
D_{k}
. Furthermore, noting that
k=D\tilde{D}_{k}D^{A}_{1}=\tilde{D}\tilde{D}_{k}D^{A}_{1}
, we can express the entropy of
\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}^{N}
as

\displaystyle H(\hat{\boldsymbol{F}}^{N})=

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1-\epsilon}{k}\log\left(\frac{1-\epsilon}{k}\right)-\sum_{i>k}({\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}}^{N}\otimes\boldsymbol{f}^{A}_{0}\otimes\tilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}})_{i}^{\downarrow}\log( {\hat{\v{P}}}^N \otimes\v{f}^A_0 \otimes\tilde{\v{\eta}})_{i}^{\downarrow}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(1-\epsilon)\log(1-\epsilon)+(1-\epsilon)\log k

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt-\sum_{i>k}({\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}}^{N}\otimes\boldsymbol{f}^{A}_{0}\otimes\tilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}})_{i}^{\downarrow}\log\Big(\frac{{\hat{\v{P}}}^N}{\tilde{D}D_0^A}\Big)_{i}^{\downarrow}

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\log k-\epsilon\log L-\sum_{i>L}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}^{\downarrow}\log(\hat{\v{P}}^N)_{i}^{\downarrow},

(147)

where in the last step of we used the fact that
L=k/(\tilde{D}D_{0}^{A})
, which comes from Eq. (
113
). Thus, from Eq. (
146
), the dissipated free energy in the optimal distillation process is simply bounded by

\displaystyle F^{N}_{\text{diss}}\gtrsim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}\Bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{L}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}^{\downarrow}\log(\hat{\v{P}}^N)_{i}^{\downarrow}+\log\Big(\frac{k}{\tilde{D}D^A}\Big)-\epsilon\log L-\log\gamma_{0}^{A}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}\Bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{L}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}^{\downarrow}\log(\hat{\v{P}}^N)_{i}^{\downarrow}+(1-\epsilon)\log L\!\Bigg].

(148)

Finally, using the expression for
L
given in Eq. (
132
), we can rewrite the above bound as

\displaystyle F^{N}_{\text{diss}}\gtrsim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}\Bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{L}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}^{\downarrow}\log(\hat{\v{P}}^N)_{i}^{\downarrow}+(1-\epsilon)\left(AN+x\sqrt{Nv_{N}}\right)\Bigg],

(149)

where
A
and
x
are given by Eq. (
133
).

Calculating bound for optimal dissipation

We now proceed to bounding the first term on the right hand side of Eq. (
149
). We start by noting that Eq. (
138
) implies that the following holds for any
\delta>0
:

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{i=1}^{L(x)}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}^{\downarrow}\log(\hat{\v{P}}^N)_{i}^{\downarrow}\geq\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{i=1}^{\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}(L(x+\delta))}\!\!\!\!(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}^{\downarrow}\log(\hat{\v{P}}^N)_{i}^{\downarrow}.

(150)

Next, from the definition of
\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}[L(x+\delta)]
, it follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}[(L(x+\delta)]}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}^{\downarrow}\log(\hat{\v{P}}^N)_{i}^{\downarrow}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\Big\{\hat{P}^{N}_{i}\log(\hat{P}^N_i)\penalty\ \Big|\penalty\ \hat{P}^{N}_{i}\geq\frac{1}{L(x+\delta)}\Big\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k},g_{\boldsymbol{k}}}\left\{\hat{P}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k},g_{\boldsymbol{k}}}\log(\hat{P}^N_{\v{k},g_{\v{k}}})\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ \hat{P}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k},g_{\boldsymbol{k}}}\geq\frac{1}{L(x+\delta)}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\left\{{P}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\log(\hat{P}^N_{\v{k},1})\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {P}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\geq\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{d}D_{i}^{k_{i}}}{L(x+\delta)}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\left\{{P}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\log({P}^N_{\v{k}})\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {P}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\geq\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{d}D_{i}^{k_{i}}}{L(x+\delta)}\right\}

\displaystyle-\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\left\{{P}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{d}k_{j}\log\gamma_{j}\Big)\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ {P}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\geq\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{d}D_{i}^{k_{i}}}{L(x+\delta)}\right\}

\displaystyle-(1-\epsilon)N\log D.

(151)

We now note that the constraint on the summand in Eq. (
151
) can be modified using the parametrization of
\boldsymbol{k}
as a function of
\boldsymbol{s}
given in Eq. (
117
) as follows:

\displaystyle\log(P^N_{\v{k}})\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}k_{i}\log D_{i}-\log(L(x+\delta))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}(Np_{i}+\sqrt{N}s_{i})\log\gamma_{i}+N\log D-\log L(x+\delta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\beta\sqrt{N}\sum_{i=1}^{d}s_{i}E_{i}+N\Big(\log D-D(\psi\|\gamma)\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt-N\Big(\log D-D(\psi\|\gamma)\Big)-(x+\delta)\sqrt{Nv_{N}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\beta\sqrt{N}\sum_{i=1}^{d}s_{i}E_{i}-(x+\delta)\sqrt{Nv_{N}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\beta\left(\sqrt{N}\boldsymbol{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{E}+\delta\sigma(F^{N})+\Delta F^{N}\right),

(152)

where in the second equality we used the form of
L
given in Eq. (
132
) and employed Eq. (
118
), whereas in the final equality we used the definition
\sigma(F^{N})=\sqrt{Nv_{n}}/\beta
and Eq. (
133
) saying that
x=\Delta F^{N}/\sigma(F^{N})
. Moreover, since
\log(P^N_{\v{k}(\v{s})})=O(\log N)
, the condition from Eq. (
152
) can be rewritten as

\sqrt{N}\boldsymbol{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{E}+\Delta F^{N}+\delta\sigma(F^{N})\gtrsim 0.

(153)

Coming back, we can now rewrite Eq. (
151
) using the parametrization of
\boldsymbol{k}
as a function of
\boldsymbol{s}
from Eq. (
117
) and re-expressing the constraint using Eq. (
153
):

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\chi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N}}(L(x+\delta))}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}^{\downarrow}\log(\hat{\v{P}}^N)_{i}^{\downarrow}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s})}\Bigg\{{P}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s})}\log({P}^N_{\v{k}(\v{s})})-\sqrt{N}{P}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s})}\sum_{i=1}^{d}s_{i}\log\gamma_{i}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\Bigg|\sqrt{N}\boldsymbol{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{E}+\Delta F^{N}+\delta\sigma(F^{N})\geq 0\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\hskip 142.26378pt-(1-\epsilon)N\Big(\log D-D(\psi\|\gamma)\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\simeq\beta\sqrt{N}\sum_{\boldsymbol{s}}\Bigg\{{P}^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s})}\boldsymbol{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{E}\Bigg|\sqrt{N}\boldsymbol{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{E}+\Delta F^{N}+\delta\sigma(F^{N})\geq 0\Bigg\}-(1-\epsilon)AN,

(154)

where we used the definition of
A
from Eq. (
133
).

Our next goal goal is then to calculate the sum

\sum_{\boldsymbol{s}}\Big\{P^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s})}\boldsymbol{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{E}\bigg|\sqrt{N}\boldsymbol{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{E}+\Delta F^{N}+\delta\sigma(F^{N})\geq 0\Big\}.

(155)

Similarly as before, we will approximate the multinomial distribution
\boldsymbol{P}^{N}
by a multivariate normal distribution
\mathcal{N}^{(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}
with mean vector
\boldsymbol{\mu}=N\boldsymbol{p}
and covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=N(\text{diag }(\boldsymbol{p})-\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{T})
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{d}|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}({\boldsymbol{k}}-{\boldsymbol{\mu}})^{T}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{k}}-{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{d}|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{s}^{T}{N\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1}\boldsymbol{s}\right).

(156)

Next, we standardise the multivariate normal distribution
\mathcal{N}^{(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}
using rotation and scaling transformations:

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\Theta^{T}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}\Theta,

(157)

where
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}
is a diagonal matrix with the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
and
\Theta
is an orthogonal matrix with columns given by the eigenvectors of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
. This rotation and scaling of co-ordinates allows us to write
\mathcal{N}^{(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}
as a product of univariate standard normal distribution
\phi(y_{i})
:

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\mathcal{N}^{(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{y}))}

\displaystyle\!=\!\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{d}|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}|}}\exp\left(\!-\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{y}^{T}\boldsymbol{y}\!\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\phi(y_{i}),\!

(158)

where

\boldsymbol{y}=\sqrt{N}(\Theta^{T}\sqrt{\Lambda})^{-1}\boldsymbol{s},

(159)

such that

\boldsymbol{s}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\Theta^{T}\sqrt{\Lambda}\boldsymbol{y}.

(160)

From Eq. (
159
) and Eq. (
160
), one can write equivalently the sum given in Eq. (
155
) as

\sum_{\boldsymbol{y}}\Big\{P^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{y}))}\frac{\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}\cdot\boldsymbol{y}}{\sqrt{N}}\bigg|\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}\cdot\boldsymbol{y}+\Delta F^{N}+\delta\sigma(F^{N})\geq 0\Big\},

(161)

where we defined
\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}:=\sqrt{\Lambda}\Theta\boldsymbol{E}
with the following normalisation

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}\|

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}}=\sqrt{(\sqrt{\Lambda}\Theta\boldsymbol{E})\cdot(\sqrt{\Lambda}\Theta\boldsymbol{E})}=\sqrt{\boldsymbol{E}\cdot\Theta^{T}\Lambda\Theta\boldsymbol{E}}=\sqrt{\boldsymbol{E}\cdot\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{E}}

\displaystyle=\sigma(F^{N}).

(162)

One can then equivalently express Eq. (
161
) as

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma(F^{N})}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{y}}\Big\{P^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{y}))}\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{y}\;\bigg|\;

\displaystyle\sigma(F^{N})\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{y}+\Delta F^{N}+\delta\sigma(F^{N})\geq 0\Big\},

(163)

where
\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}}
is unit vector along
\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}
. Now, we choose a rotation
R
such that

R\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}}=(1,\ldots,0)^{T}

(164)

and we call
R\boldsymbol{y}=\boldsymbol{x}
. Thus, we can rewrite Eq. (
163
) as

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma(F^{N})}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{y}}\Big\{P^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{y}))}R\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}}\cdot R\boldsymbol{y}\;\Bigg|\;\sigma(F^{N})R\hat{\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}}\cdot R\boldsymbol{y}+\Delta F^{N}+\delta\sigma(F^{N})\geq 0\Big\}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt=\frac{\sigma(F^{N})}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{x}}\Big\{P^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s}(\boldsymbol{x}))}x_{1}\;\Bigg|\;\sigma(F^{N})x_{1}+\Delta F^{N}+\delta\sigma(F^{N})\geq 0\Big\}.

(165)

Now, employing multivariate normal distribution approximation and the fact that it is isotropic, we can exactly calculate the above expression as an integral over
\boldsymbol{x}
of the following form

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma(F^{N})}{\sqrt{N}}\Bigg(\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}dx_{2}\phi(x_{2})\ldots\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}dx_{d}

\displaystyle\phi(x_{d})\int_{-\infty}^{d_{\mathcal{O}}}dx_{1}x_{1}\phi(x_{1})\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma(F^{N})}{\sqrt{2\pi N}}\exp\left(-\frac{d_{\mathcal{O}}^{2}}{2}\right),

(166)

where
d_{\mathcal{O}}
is the distance between the origin and the hyperplane specified by the constraint given in Eq. (
165
):

d_{\mathcal{O}}=\frac{\Delta F^{N}+\delta\sigma(F^{N})}{\sqrt{\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}}}=\frac{\Delta F^{N}}{\sigma(F^{N})}+\delta.

(167)

Thus, finally we get the following expression for the desired sum from Eq. (
155
):

\displaystyle\sum_{\boldsymbol{s}}\Big\{P^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s})}\boldsymbol{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{E}\bigg|\sqrt{N}\boldsymbol{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{E}

\displaystyle+\Delta F^{N}+\delta\sigma(F^{N})\geq 0\Big\}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma(F^{N})}{\sqrt{2\pi N}}\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\Delta F^{N}}{\sigma(F^{N})}+\delta\right)^{2}\right].

(168)

Substituting the above to Eq. (
154
), and the resulting expression to Eq. (
150
), we get the following bound:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{L(x)}(\hat{\boldsymbol{P}}^{N})_{i}^{\downarrow}\log(\hat{\v{P}}^N)_{i}^{\downarrow}\gtrsim-(1-\epsilon)A+\frac{\beta\sigma(F^{N})}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\Delta F^{N}}{\sigma(F^{N})}+\delta\right)^{2}\right],

(169)

which in turn can be used in Eq. (
149
) to give the following:

\displaystyle F^{N}_{\mathrm{diss}}\gtrsim

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma(F^{N})}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\Delta F^{N}}{\sigma(F^{N})}+\delta\right)^{2}\right]+(1-\epsilon)\Delta F^{N}.

(170)

Since the above holds for any
\delta>0
, by taking the limit
\delta\rightarrow 0
, we finally arrive at

F^{N}_{\text{diss}}\gtrsim(1-\epsilon)\Delta F^{N}+\frac{\sigma(F^{N})}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp[{-\frac{(\Delta F^{N})^{2}}{2\sigma(F^{N})^{2}}}\Bigg].

(171)

Employing Eq. (
141
) we have

\Delta F^{N}=-\Phi^{-1}(\epsilon)\sigma(F^{N}),

(172)

and so substituting this in Eq. (
171
) we obtain

\displaystyle F^{N}_{\text{diss}}

\displaystyle a(\epsilon)\sigma(F^{N}),

(173)

with
a
given by Eq. (
13
), which completes the proof.

Subsection 6
Optimality of the communication rate

The following derivation will closely follow the proof of Lemma 1 of Ref.
[
141
]
. Let us assume that for a system
(\rho^{N},H^{N})
it is possible to encode
M
messages in a thermodynamically-free way so that the average decoding error is
\epsilon
. It means that there exists a set of
M
encoding thermal operations
\{\mathcal{E}^{\beta}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{M}
and a decoding POVM
\{\Pi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{M}
such that

1-\epsilon=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\tr{\E^{\beta}_i(\rho^N) \Pi_i}.

(174)

Note that every thermal operation
\mathcal{E}^{\beta}_{i}
between the initial system
(\rho^{N},H^{N})
and a target system
(\tilde{\rho}^{N},\tilde{H}^{N})
preserves the thermal equilibrium state, i.e.,

\mathcal{E}^{\beta}_{i}(\gamma^{N})=\tilde{\gamma}^{N}.

(175)

Now, let us introduce the following three states

\displaystyle\tau:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\outerproduct{i}{i}\otimes\mathcal{E}^{\beta}_{i}(\rho^{N}),

(176a)

\displaystyle\zeta:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\outerproduct{i}{i}\otimes\gamma^{N},

(176b)

\displaystyle\tilde{\zeta}:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\outerproduct{i}{i}\otimes\tilde{\gamma}^{N}.

(176c)

The hypothesis testing relative entropy
D_{H}^{\epsilon}
between
\tau
and
\tilde{\zeta}
, defined by
[
235
,
236
,
237
]

\displaystyle D_{H}^{\epsilon}(\tau\|\tilde{\zeta})\!:=-\log\inf\big\{\tr{Q\tilde{\zeta}}\ \big|\

\displaystyle 0\leq Q\leq\mathbbm{1},\tr{Q\tau}\geq 1-\epsilon\big\},

(177)

satisfies the following

D_{H}^{\epsilon}(\tau\|\tilde{\zeta})\geq-\log\tr{Q\tilde{\zeta}}

(178)

for

Q=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\outerproduct{i}{i}\otimes\Pi_{i}.

(179)

This is because the above (potentially suboptimal) choice of
Q
clearly satisfies
0\leq Q\leq\mathbbm{1}
and also

\tr{Q\tau}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\tr{\E^{\beta}_i(\rho)\Pi_i}\geq 1-\epsilon,

(180)

due to our assumption in Eq. (
174
). At the same time we have

\tr{Q\tilde{\zeta}}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\tr{\tilde{\rho}^N \Pi_i}=\frac{1}{M},

(181)

so that

\log M\leq D_{H}^{\epsilon}(\tau\|\tilde{\zeta}).

(182)

Next, we introduce the following encoding channel

\mathcal{F}:=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\outerproduct{i}{i}\otimes\mathcal{E}^{\beta}_{i},

(183)

which satisfies

\mathcal{F}(\zeta)=\tilde{\zeta}.

(184)

Employing the data-processing inequality twice, we get the following sequence of inequalities:

\displaystyle D_{H}^{\epsilon}(\tau\|\tilde{\zeta})

\displaystyle=D_{H}^{\epsilon}\left[\mathcal{F}\left(\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\outerproduct{i}{i}\otimes\rho^{N}\right)\bigg\|\mathcal{F}({\zeta})\right]

\displaystyle\leq D_{H}^{\epsilon}\left(\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\outerproduct{i}{i}\otimes\rho^{N}\bigg\|\zeta\right)

\displaystyle\leq D_{H}^{\epsilon}\big(\rho^{N}\big\|\gamma^{N}\big).

(185)

Combining this with Eq. (
182
), we arrive at

\log M\leq D_{H}^{\epsilon}\big(\rho^{N}\big\|\gamma^{N}\big).

(186)

Finally, for the case of identical initial subsystems,
\rho^{N}=\rho^{\otimes N}
and
\gamma^{N}=\gamma^{\otimes N}
, we can use the known second order asymptotic expansion of the hypothesis testing relative entropy
[
238
]
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}D^{\epsilon}_{H}(\rho^{\otimes N}\|\gamma^{\otimes N})

\displaystyle\simeq D(\rho\|\gamma)+\sqrt{\frac{V(\rho\|\gamma)}{N}}\Phi^{-1}(\epsilon),

(187)

leading to

\displaystyle\frac{\log M}{N}\lesssim D(\rho\|\gamma)+\sqrt{\frac{V(\rho\|\gamma)}{N}}\Phi^{-1}(\epsilon).

(188)

For the above proof to work also in the case of non-identical subsystems, one would need to prove the following asymptotic behaviour of the hypothesis testing relative entropy:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}D^{\epsilon}_{H}\left(\bigotimes_{n=1}^{N}\rho_{n}\bigg\|\bigotimes_{n=1}^{N}\gamma_{n}\right)\simeq\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{n=1}^{N}D(\rho_{n}\|\gamma_{n})+\sqrt{\frac{\frac{1}{N}\!\sum\limits_{n=1}^{N}\!V(\rho_{n}\|\gamma_{n})}{N}}\Phi^{-1}(\epsilon).\!

(189)

Subsection 7
Proof of Lemma
5.4

Consider
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}
to be a probability vector that saturates
\boldsymbol{p}\succ_{\epsilon}\boldsymbol{q}
. By definition it means that
\boldsymbol{p}\succ\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}
and
F(\boldsymbol{q},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})=1-\epsilon
. Since
V(\boldsymbol{q})=0
and
H(\boldsymbol{q})=\log L
, the probability vector
\boldsymbol{q}
contains exactly
L
non-zero entries such that each of them is equal to
1/L
. Thus,

\boldsymbol{q}^{\downarrow}=\Big(\underbrace{\frac{1}{L},\ldots,\frac{1}{L}}_{L},0,\ldots,0\Big).

(190)

From the definition of
F(\boldsymbol{q},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})
given in Eq. (
33
) we have the following

\displaystyle 1-\epsilon=F(\boldsymbol{q},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle F(\boldsymbol{q}^{\downarrow},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\downarrow})

(191)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{L}\sqrt{\tilde{q}_{i}^{\downarrow}}\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{L}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{L}\sqrt{\tilde{q}_{i}^{\downarrow}\cdot 1}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{L}\tilde{q}_{i}^{\downarrow}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{L}1\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{L}\tilde{q}_{i}^{\downarrow},

where the first inequality follows from the definition of fidelity, while the second one comes from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Now, from Lemma
5.4
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{L}p_{i}^{\downarrow}=1-\epsilon.

(192)

Combining this with Eq. (
191
) we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{L}p_{i}^{\downarrow}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{L}\tilde{q}_{i}^{\downarrow}.

(193)

On the other hand,
\boldsymbol{p}\succ\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}
gives

\sum_{i=1}^{L}p_{i}^{\downarrow}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{L}\tilde{q}_{i}^{\downarrow},

(194)

and so we conclude that

\sum_{i=1}^{L}p_{i}^{\downarrow}=\sum_{i=1}^{L}\tilde{q}_{i}^{\downarrow}=1-\epsilon.

(195)

Next, note that as
\epsilon=1-F(\boldsymbol{q},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})\geq 1-F(\boldsymbol{q}^{\downarrow},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\downarrow})
, we see that to have the minimal value of error,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\downarrow}
needs to maximize the fidelity
F(\boldsymbol{q}^{\downarrow},\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\downarrow})
subject to the constraint given in Eq. (
195
). Thus we can write

\begin{split}&\max\penalty\ \penalty\ \qquad\frac{1}{L}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{L}\sqrt{\tilde{q}_{i}^{\downarrow}}\right)^{2}\\
&\textrm{such that}\quad\sum_{i=1}^{L}\tilde{q}_{i}^{\downarrow}=1-\epsilon.\end{split}

(196)

The Lagrangian of the aforementioned optimization problem in Eq. (
196
) is given by

\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{L}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{L}\sqrt{\tilde{q}_{i}^{\downarrow}}\right)^{2}-\lambda\left(\sum_{i=1}^{L}\tilde{q}_{i}^{\downarrow}-1+\epsilon\right),

(197)

where
\lambda
is a Lagrange multiplier. To find the solution of the problem we calculate

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\tilde{q}_{j}^{\downarrow}}=\frac{1}{\Big(L\sqrt{\tilde{q}_{j}^{\downarrow}}\Big)}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{L}\sqrt{\tilde{q}_{i}^{\downarrow}}\right)-\lambda

(198)

for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
. Solving
\partial\mathcal{L}/\partial\tilde{q}_{j}^{\downarrow}=0
we get

\displaystyle\tilde{q}^{\downarrow}_{j}=\Bigg[\frac{1}{\lambda L}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{L}\sqrt{\tilde{q}_{i}^{\downarrow}}\right)\Bigg]^{2}

(199)

for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
. Substituting
\tilde{q}^{\downarrow}_{j}
from Eq. (
199
) to the constraint specified in Eq. (
195
) gives

\lambda=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(1-\epsilon)L}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{L}\sqrt{\tilde{q}_{i}^{\downarrow}}\right).

(200)

Therefore, putting
\lambda
from Eq. (
200
) into Eq. (
199
) finally gives

\displaystyle\quad\forall i\in\{1,\ldots,L\}:\qquad\tilde{q}_{i}^{\downarrow}=\frac{1-\epsilon}{L}.

(201)

Since fidelity is a concave function, and the constraint defined in Eq. (
196
) is linear, this solution is optimal.

Next, we note that
\boldsymbol{p}\succ\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}
implies
Q\boldsymbol{p}^{\downarrow}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\downarrow}
where
Q
is a bistochastic matrix. Since any
Q
can be decomposed as a convex sum of permutations, we can write

Q=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\Pi_{i},

(202)

where
\Pi_{i}
is a permutation matrix and
\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}=1
with
\alpha_{i}\in[0,1]
for all
i
. Let us now define a vector
\boldsymbol{v}
as

\boldsymbol{v}:=\Big(\underbrace{1,\ldots,1}_{L},0\ldots,0\Big).

(203)

Using this
\boldsymbol{v}
, we can write the following

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}\cdot Q\boldsymbol{p}^{\downarrow}=\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\downarrow}=\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{p}^{\downarrow}=(1-\epsilon)\Rightarrow\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\Pi_{i}\boldsymbol{p}^{\downarrow}=\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{p}^{\downarrow},

(204)

where we use Eq. (
195
) in the first line, and the convex decomposition of
Q
from Eq. (
202
) in the second line. Note that Eq. (
204
) can be equivalently written as

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}(\Pi_{i}^{T}\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{p}^{\downarrow}=\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{p}^{\downarrow},

(205)

where
\Pi_{i}^{T}
is the transpose of the permutation matrix
\Pi_{i}
. Because the elements of
\boldsymbol{v}
and
\boldsymbol{p}^{\downarrow}
are arranged in a decreasing order, the maximum value of
(\Pi_{i}^{T}\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{p}^{\downarrow}
is
\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{p}^{\downarrow}
. Thus, we see that equality in Eq. (
205
) holds if and only if
\boldsymbol{v}
is invariant under
(\Pi_{i}^{T})
for all
i
. Therefore,
\Pi_{i}
can be only of the form

\Pi_{i}=\Pi_{i}^{L}\oplus\Pi_{i}^{L^{c}},

(206)

where
\Pi_{i}^{L}
and
\Pi_{i}^{L^{c}}
are permutations that act trivially for indices
i>L
and
i\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
, respectively. Thus, we infer using Eq. (
202
) that
Q
is a block-diagonal matrix of the form

Q=\tilde{B}\oplus B,

(207)

where
\tilde{B}
and
B
are
L\times L
and
(d-L)\times(d-L)
bistochastic matrices. Thus,

\displaystyle\forall i>L,\quad\tilde{q}_{i}^{\downarrow}=\sum_{j>L}B_{ij}p_{j}^{\downarrow}.

(208)

Combining Eq. (
201
) and Eq. (
208
) we conclude that
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}^{\downarrow}={\boldsymbol{q}}^{*\downarrow}
with
\boldsymbol{q}^{*}
defined in Eq. (
74
). This finally implies
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}=\Pi\boldsymbol{q}^{*}
with
\Pi
being some permutation. Moreover, it is straightforward to show that the minimal entropy of
\boldsymbol{q}^{*}
is achieved for
B
being the identity matrix, since the entropy is increasing under application of any non-trivial bistochastic matrix.

Subsection 8
Eliminating the logarithmic term

We start with the following lemma that will be needed to prove our claim.

Lemma 5.6

(Logarithmic growth of probability)
.

For a fixed value of
b>0
and any
\boldsymbol{s}
, such that
\|\boldsymbol{s}\|=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{d}s_{i}^{2}}\leq b
, we have

\log P^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s})}=O(\log N),

(209)

where
P^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
and
\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s})
are defined by Eqs. (
107
) and (
117
), respectively.

Proof.

We start by using the definition,

P^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s})}=\binom{N}{k_{1}(s_{1}),...,k_{d}(s_{d})}p^{k_{1}(s_{1})}_{1}...p^{k_{d}(s_{d})}_{d},

(210)

to write
\log P^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s})}
as

\!\!\!\log P^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s})}\!=\!\log N!-\sum_{i=1}^{d}\log k_{i}(s_{i})!+\sum_{i=1}^{d}k_{i}(s_{i})\log p_{i}.\!

(211)

Employing Stirling inequality,

\displaystyle\log\sqrt{N}+N\log N-N\leq\log N!\leq 1+\log\sqrt{N}+N\log N-N,

(212)

we first provide a lower bound for
\log P^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s})}
,

\displaystyle\log P^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s})}\geq

\displaystyle(\log\sqrt{N}+N\log N-N)+\sum_{i=1}^{d}k_{i}(s_{i})\log p_{i}

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{d}(1\!+\!\log\sqrt{k_{i}(s_{i})}\!+\!k_{i}(s_{i})\log k_{i}(s_{i})\!-\!k_{i}(s_{i}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{d}k_{i}(s_{i})\log\Big(\frac{k_i(s_i)}{Np_i}\Big)-d+\frac{1}{2}\Big[\log N-\sum_{i=1}^{d}\log k_{i}(s_{i})\Big].

(213)

Recall that
k_{i}(s_{i})=Np_{i}+\sqrt{N}s_{i}
, which implies
\sum_{i=1}^{d}s_{i}=0
. To simplify the above further, we lower bound the first term by employing the inequality
\log(1+g)<g
for
g>-1
in the following way:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}k_{i}(s_{i})\log\Big(\frac{k_i(s_i)}{Np_i}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}k_{i}(s_{i})\log\left(1+\frac{s_{i}}{\sqrt{N}p_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d}k_{i}(s_{i})\frac{s_{i}}{\sqrt{N}p_{i}}=\sqrt{N}\sum_{i=1}^{d}(1+\frac{s_{i}}{\sqrt{N}p_{i}})s_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{s_{i}^{2}}{p_{i}}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{s_{i}^{2}}{p_{\min}}\leq\frac{b^{2}}{p_{\min}},

(214)

where
p_{\min}=\text{min }\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{d}\}
. Moreover, observing that

\log N\geq\left(\log N-\sum_{i=1}^{d}\log k_{i}(s_{i})\right)\geq-(d-1)\log N,

(215)

we can conclude that

\log N-\sum_{i=1}^{d}\log k_{i}(s_{i})=O(\log N).

(216)

Putting it together, we further simplify the bound given in Eq. (
213
) as

\!\!\!\log P^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s})}\geq-d-\frac{b^{2}}{p_{\min}}-\frac{(d-1)}{2}\log{N}=O(\log N).

(217)

Similarly, by employing the Stirling inequality from Eq. (
212
), we also prove an upper bound for
\log P^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s})}
as follows

\displaystyle\log P^{N}_{\boldsymbol{k}(\boldsymbol{s})}\leq

\displaystyle(1+\log{\sqrt{N}}+N\log N-N)+\sum_{i=1}^{d}k_{i}(s_{i})\log p_{i}

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{d}\Big(\log\sqrt{k_{i}(s_{i})}+k_{i}(s_{i})\log k_{i}(s_{i})-k_{i}(s_{i})\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{d}k_{i}(s_{i})\log\Big(\frac{k_i(s_i)}{Np_i}\Big)+1+\frac{1}{2}(\log N-\sum_{i=1}^{d}\log k_{i}(s_{i})).

(218)

Using the inequality
\log(1+g)\geq\frac{g}{1+g}
for
g>-1
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}k_{i}(s_{i})\log\Big(\frac{k_i(s_i)}{Np_i}\Big)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}k_{i}(s_{i})\log\Big(1+\frac{s_i}{\sqrt{N}p_i}\Big)
